## [Multi-Agent Code Judges Need Grounded Evidence, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30328) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper argues that multi-agent verification of code requires evidence that is independent of the answer under review and that differs between the two candidates being compared. Running the published MARCH framework unmodified over 80 condition-by-cell measurements on two code judging benchmarks, the authors found it declares both solutions equally good in 78–95% of comparisons, achieving only 4.4% accuracy versus 43.7% for the same model asked directly. The finding exposes a serious failure mode in multi-agent code judging, a technique increasingly used by software teams to let one model write code and another decide whether it is correct enough to merge. The paper's label-free measurements offer a way to detect when a judge has no real basis for its verdict, which matters for AI safety and reliable code verification. The second condition—evidence differing between candidates—holds automatically when evidence is a set of retrieved documents but stops holding in code judging, since both candidate solutions are typically checked against the same tests. Neither easier problems nor a larger judge model changes the outcome; gating on one of the two label-free measurements lets the pipeline decline comparisons it cannot make, raising accuracy from 20.7% to 36.9% while still answering half of all comparisons.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent verification decomposes a judgment into checkable claims and verifies each against evidence, and it works well when that evidence is a set of retrieved documents, as in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). MARCH (Multi-Agent Reinforced Check for Hallucination) is a published framework that separates answer generation from answer verification and intentionally creates information asymmetry between agents to reduce hallucination. The paper's contribution is not a more accurate judge but a label-free way to tell when a judge has no basis for its answer.

## [Skill Cascading Attacks Threaten Skill-Based LLM Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30383) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30383) introduces "skill cascading attacks," a threat paradigm in which a malicious objective is split across multiple skills so each modification looks benign alone but becomes harmful when combined. The authors release SkillCascade, an automated multi-agent red-teaming framework, and SkillCascade-Bench, a benchmark of 213 validated cascading test cases across multiple agent systems and domains. As LLM agents increasingly load third-party skills at runtime, this work exposes a gap between component-level integrity and system-level safety, showing that per-skill scanners and runtime monitors can be evaded. It affects anyone building or deploying skill-based agent systems, from platforms like Claude Code and Codex to enterprise pipelines such as prescription review. The paper demonstrates the attack on representative agents including OpenClaw, Claude Code, and Codex across multiple LLM backbones, with a concrete example in a prescription-review pipeline where three skills successively weaken discontinued-medication signals, downgrade drug-interaction severity, and suppress the final alert. Notably, it is an arXiv preprint without peer review, and the benchmark's 213 cases are validated but limited to the domains the authors tested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Skills are modular packages of natural-language instructions, executable scripts, and reference resources that an agent can load at runtime to extend its capabilities, similar to Claude's Agent Skills format. Because these skills are often third-party and openly shared, they create a new attack surface; prior security work focused on vulnerabilities inside individual skills, not on interactions between them. Cascading attacks exploit this blind spot by distributing a harmful objective across several seemingly benign skills.

Existing evaluations often measure fidelity—how well an explanation reproduces the black-box model's output—but high fidelity does not guarantee the explanation reflects the true underlying decision process. Synthetic datasets with known ground truth, such as those built via controlled interventions, offer a way to test explanations more reliably.

## [Spectral Feedback Aligns Protein Diffusion Models at Test Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30456) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30456) introduces Spectral Feedback, a test-time alignment algorithm for discrete diffusion models that iteratively re-masks and re-samples tokens in a feedback loop, treating edit-position selection as the central alignment problem. Applied to protein inverse folding with a stability reward oracle, it increases stable proteins by 32.3% for a pretrained model, 24.8% for Best-of-10, and 5.8% for a state-of-the-art RL fine-tuned diffusion model. Most reward-maximization alignment methods for discrete diffusion models steer the reverse process in a unidirectional way, with no mechanism to revisit bad token choices; Spectral Feedback adds that missing correction loop and is model-agnostic, so it can be layered on top of pretrained, test-time aligned, or fine-tuned models without changing the underlying generative process. This could make protein design pipelines more reliable by squeezing extra reward out of existing models rather than retraining them. The key insight is that edit effects are interdependent — the impact of changing one token depends on which other tokens are edited at the same time — so the algorithm defines an edit-set and learns its value function. Motivated by sparse interactions in biological systems, the authors find empirically that edit-set value functions for protein inverse folding admit sparse Fourier representations, which makes learning and optimizing edit-position selection efficient.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Discrete diffusion models are generative models that corrupt data with discrete noise and learn to reverse that process, and they have become a promising alternative to autoregressive models for text and other discrete data. Protein diffusion models such as RFdiffusion generate protein structures and sequences by reversing a noising process, and inverse folding is the task of predicting an amino-acid sequence that folds into a given structure. Test-time alignment improves model outputs at inference without updating weights, but prior methods for discrete diffusion mainly steer token logits or select favorable intermediate sequences rather than revisiting earlier choices.

## [LLM-as-a-Judge Filtering Improves Pseudo-Labeled ASR for Noisy Police Radio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30469) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30469) systematically evaluates pseudo-labeling for adapting pretrained ASR models (Whisper and Qwen3-ASR) to noisy Broadcast Police Communication (BPC) corpora from Baltimore and Chicago. It shows that internal confidence metrics (log-probabilities and STAR scores) fail to distinguish high- from low-quality pseudo-labels, and introduces an external LLM-as-a-judge filter that discards contextually implausible transcripts, significantly reducing word error rate (WER). This work addresses a real-world bottleneck: pretrained ASR systems perform poorly on noisy police radio, hindering computational social science research into police decision-making. By showing that an external LLM judge outperforms internal confidence metrics, it offers a practical, label-efficient path to domain adaptation and points to a promising cross-model pseudo-labeling direction. The LLM judge filters more aggressively than internal metrics and significantly reduces WER across both the Baltimore and Chicago BPC corpora, though a substantial gap remains compared to an oracle filter. The paper also introduces a cross-model pseudo-labeling paradigm where one model is finetuned using pseudo-labels generated by the other, identified as a promising direction for future work.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) systems such as OpenAI's Whisper are trained on massive web-collected audio, but they degrade sharply on specialized noisy domains like police radio. ## [Knowledge Graph Framework Evaluates LLM Contextual Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30484) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30484) proposes a knowledge graph-based evaluation framework for assessing whether large language models truly understand context in question answering, rather than just performing surface-level pattern matching. The framework introduces Semantic Structural Similarity for KGs (S3KG), a hybrid measure combining structural and semantic signals, and achieves F1 gains of up to +7.6 points over the strongest baseline and AUROC up to 0.973 across nine benchmarks. Traditional metrics like BLEU and perplexity only measure surface-level performance and cannot verify whether a model's answer is contextually grounded, leaving a critical gap in QA evaluation. This framework offers a more diagnostic approach that could help researchers and practitioners better identify reasoning failures and build more trustworthy LLMs. The framework constructs knowledge graphs from both model-generated responses and gold answers, then compares them using S3KG, which combines structural and semantic signals into a single score. It also includes a diagnostic analysis framework that identifies and categorizes reasoning errors at the triplet level, enabling fine-grained analysis of model failures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved impressive linguistic capabilities, but it remains unclear whether they genuinely comprehend context or simply excel at pattern matching. Traditional evaluation metrics such as BLEU (Bilingual Evaluation Understudy) and perplexity measure surface-level similarity or prediction uncertainty, not factual consistency or contextual grounding. Knowledge graphs represent entities and their relationships as structured triplets, making them a natural tool for verifying whether a model's answer captures the same relational structure as a correct answer.

## [Benchy proposes a universal language for AI benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30550) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30550v1) introduces Benchy, a semantic language and execution engine that specifies an AI benchmark as a triple B=(P,S,D) of program, scoring function, and dataset, fully separate from the AI system being tested. Benchmarks are authored in canonical YAML, classified by a shared task/domain/language ontology, and deterministically compiled into a canonical JSON intermediate representation that the engine executes. AI evaluation today suffers from poor reproducibility and interoperability, since every benchmark bundles its own integration code with its scoring logic. By fixing one universal runtime contract — a named-field input object in, a named-field output object out — Benchy keeps integration mechanics from leaking into benchmark semantics, which could make benchmarks portable, comparable, and easier to reuse across models, hosted endpoints, and task-specific pipelines. Compilation only changes representation, not meaning: the engine does not repair invalid definitions or inject hidden defaults, and programs use fixed schemas of named input and output fields whose leaf output fields become the scoring dimensions. The paper covers the semantic object model, the ontology and task-to-program validation rule, scoring and failure semantics, the compilation and execution architecture, and the current language scope, with an appendix fixing the normative engineering contract for the first engine implementation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Benchmarks for AI systems are typically ad-hoc scripts that mix dataset loading, prompt formatting, model invocation, and scoring, which makes results hard to reproduce or compare across systems. Benchy draws on ideas from programming-language design — canonical syntax, ontologies, deterministic compilation to an intermediate representation, and a stable runtime interface — and applies them to benchmark specification. The project also has an open-source implementation, the surus-lat/benchy benchmarking engine, for evaluating AI systems on task-specific performance.

## [Intuitive Prompting Boosts LLM Agents' Social Media Reaction Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30563) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30563) profiled eight Serbian participants via questionnaire, deep interview, and written self-presentation, then had four language models predict their reactions to 68 social media posts under five prompt conditions. Attitudinal profile content beat demographic backstories by a wide margin, and instructing models to respond intuitively and immediately rather than analytically achieved the highest fidelity, cutting the compression of individual differences from seven times the human level to three. The findings suggest LLM agents can serve as general-purpose simulated users rather than topic specialists, which matters for platform policy testing, social simulation, and concerns about fake profiles manipulating perceived public opinion before elections. It also implies intuition-based prompting setups may be better suited to some tasks than reasoning-based ones in language model development. The study used only eight participants and 68 posts, and the intuitive-prompting advantage held even on posts about topics the questionnaire never raised, beating a crowd baseline by a wide margin. Agents matched their stated profiles more closely than participants matched their own survey answers, and consistency proved unrelated to fidelity once profile information was present.

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly used as artificial users to test platform policies, making agent fidelity important. Prior validation has focused on agreement with human behavior but paid little attention to whether an agent behaves in line with the profile it was given. Attitudinal data captures beliefs and feelings, going deeper than demographic data, and this study compares attitudinal versus demographic profile content under different instruction styles.

## [HARDEN uses constrained evolutionary search to build harder, answer-preserving LLM benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30571) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HARDEN introduces a constrained evolutionary search method that rewrites the inputs of existing evaluation cases into harder variants while keeping their expected outputs fixed. Across FinQA, PubMedQA, and ContractNLI with three Qwen3.5 model scales (35B-A3B, 122B-A10B, 397B-A17B), it lowers task-model accuracy by 22.7% on average and up to 49.9% versus single-pass baselines using the same feasibility checks. Curated benchmarks often underrepresent the complexity of real enterprise deployments, so models can look stronger than they are. HARDEN shows that evolutionary search can generate substantially harder yet still valid evaluation cases, giving researchers and practitioners a more realistic way to stress-test model robustness without changing the ground-truth answers. HARDEN searches along generated domain-specific complexity axes while enforcing feasibility constraints such as preserving task semantics, realism, and execution validity. The reported accuracy drops are relative to single-pass baselines that apply the same feasibility checks, which helps isolate the benefit of the evolutionary search itself.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Evolutionary search is an optimization approach that iteratively mutates and selects candidate solutions, and here it is constrained so that generated cases remain semantically valid and realistic. FinQA is a financial question-answering benchmark requiring numerical reasoning over reports, PubMedQA is a biomedical question-answering benchmark, and ContractNLI is a document-level natural language inference dataset for contract review. Qwen3.5 is a family of large language models evaluated at three parameter scales in this work.

## [T-RoPE: Time-Aware Rotary Position Embedding for Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30576) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose T-RoPE, a time-aware variant of Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) for sequential generative recommendation that replaces index-only rotation with timestamp-based angles, learnable temporal coefficients, multiscale frequency banks, shifted query alignment, and non-stationary key rotation. They prove that standard RoPE remains time-translation invariant even when applied to timestamps, and show T-RoPE breaks this invariance while preserving the RoPE interface, achieving state-of-the-art results on five public benchmarks and an industrial dataset with over 6B interactions. Sequential recommenders increasingly borrow the Transformer recipe from large language models, but standard RoPE only encodes event order and ignores elapsed time, behavioral cycles, and calendar phase, which are crucial for recommendation. T-RoPE's demonstrated gains, including a 78–130% HR@10 improvement on sparse PixelRec data and positive online A/B lifts in the Shop app, suggest that time-aware position embeddings could become a standard component of large-scale generative recommenders. T-RoPE adds forward and backward algorithms whose computational cost is linear in sequence length and head dimension, keeping it practical for large generative recommenders. Ablations attribute the largest gains to multiscale frequencies (+56% NDCG@50) and non-stationary keys (+4%), and the online A/B test shows conversion rate up 0.33% and order count up 0.63%.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE), introduced with RoFormer, encodes token positions by applying a rotation matrix to queries and keys, unifying absolute and relative position information and becoming a standard component in modern Transformer language models. Sequential recommendation models predict the next item a user will interact with based on their interaction history, and as they adopt Transformer architectures, they inherit design choices like RoPE that were originally made for text. However, in recommendation an interaction index only records event order, not elapsed time or periodic behavior, motivating the need for time-aware position encoding.

## [Audio LLMs Can't Self-Judge Transcription, But Encoders Can](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30625) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30625) shows that Audio LLMs are poor judges of their own transcription reliability, almost always predicting their transcriptions will be reliable even when they are not. However, the researchers found that the frozen audio-encoder representations strongly encode transcription reliability, and a lightweight predictor built on these representations achieves 81.10% in-domain and 78.09% cross-domain macro-F1, beating the strongest baselines by 10.33 and 11.93 points. This matters because speech-based AI systems that silently act on bad transcriptions can misinterpret user queries, and a reliable pre-generation reliability signal enables the model to ask for clarification instead. The finding could improve robustness in deployed audio LLM assistants and spur further work on uncertainty estimation and interpretability for speech models. The predictor operates on representations from the frozen audio encoder before generation, so it can trigger a clarification request without modifying the underlying Audio LLM. The paper also shows reliability labels transfer across Audio LLM families, with transfer performance tied to how well their model-specific reliability boundaries align.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Audio LLMs let users interact with a model through speech, typically by encoding audio into representations that a language model then uses to generate text responses. When input recordings are degraded, the model may transcribe incorrectly and respond based on that wrong transcription. Word Error Rate (WER) is the standard metric for transcription errors, and prior reliability approaches include speech quality predictors, generation uncertainty, and transcript-conditioned WER estimation.

## [Paper Diagnoses 'LLM Parkinsonism' and Proposes Global Executive Control Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30662) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30662) introduces the concept of 'LLM Parkinsonism'—a failure mode where autonomous LLM agents keep acting after their objective is met, producing low-value refinements and repeated verification—and proposes Global Executive Control (GEC) v0.2, an uncertainty-aware governance architecture that separates action generation from project-level control. In a 24,000-episode matched-candidate benchmark under a 40,000-token ceiling, GEC matched a candidate-set control's 96.57% hard-goal success while cutting mean token use from 19,782 to 12,574 (36.4%) and eliminating measured pre-completion drift. This work targets an under-explored but practically costly failure mode in autonomous agents: persistent, low-value action that wastes tokens and compute even when the task is already solved. If validated on live models, the GEC separation of governance from generation could inform how agent frameworks handle scope, stopping criteria, and resource budgets, affecting both AI safety research and the economics of deploying long-horizon agents. The paper argues the problem is not merely autoregressive next-token prediction but the concentration of proposal generation, scope interpretation, progress assessment, and stopping authority within one self-conditioned loop. The results are from mechanistic simulations rather than live models, and the authors explicitly state that live-model validation remains necessary; governance-overhead sensitivity stayed favorable through an additional 500 synthetic governance tokens per cycle.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are systems that use a large language model to plan, call tools, write code, and execute multi-step workflows over long horizons. A 'self-conditioned loop' means the agent's own prior outputs become the context for its next decisions, which can reinforce continued action even when it is no longer useful. 'LLM Parkinsonism' is used here as a deliberately narrow, non-clinical metaphor for this persistent-action pattern, and the paper does not claim any biological homology.

## [Study: More Test-Time Reasoning Doesn't Improve LLM Trading Returns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30705) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A controlled arXiv study (2609.30705) tested representative LLMs from the DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini families across a full year of U.S. equities, varying reasoning effort while holding information, prompts, output formats, and portfolio construction fixed. Across more than 800,000 asset predictions and three input conditions (numerical, identifiable news, masked news), additional reasoning did not reliably improve net portfolio returns after trading costs. The result challenges the widely held assumption that scaling test-time compute and reasoning effort translates into better economic outcomes, and it argues that reasoning controls should be validated as economic interventions rather than assumed to help. This matters for both AI/ML researchers pushing inference-time scaling and quantitative finance practitioners considering LLM-driven trading agents. For DeepSeek, where the full progression from no reasoning to maximum reasoning was examined, performance was nonmonotonic, and repeated generations produced unstable treatment effects and portfolio selections even when overall scores looked similar. The study covers only one year of U.S. equities and three input conditions, so the negative result is scoped to this setup rather than all trading tasks.

**Background**: Inference-time reasoning (also called test-time compute scaling) refers to letting a model spend more computation while generating an answer — for example through longer chains of thought or search methods like Best-of-N — rather than changing its weights during training. This approach has become a major research direction for improving LLM reasoning on tasks such as math and coding, but its value in economic settings depends on whether better-looking outputs survive real trading costs and portfolio construction.

## [LAVOIR teaches single-pass decision encoders when and what to ask](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30706) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LAVOIR (Laya with Value-Of-Information Routing) extends single-pass decision encoders like TypeSafe's Jev and its open counterpart Laya so that one forward pass returns both the decision distribution and, for every candidate missing slot, the expected gain in the probability of a correct decision if the user were asked about it. Its VOI targets require no human labels: gold decisions come from schema rules, an LLM only verbalizes messages and answers, and a model from another family verifies every text. This gives single-pass, calibrated decision models the ability to know when a question is worth asking and which one to ask, without paying for human labels or a second inference pass. It matters for interactive assistants and structured decision systems where asking the right clarifying question can substantially raise accuracy at low latency. A Gini-impurity cap bounds the predicted value by what a calibrated model can still gain; on seen schemas decisions are statistically indistinguishable from the Bayes ceiling, the question policy matches a greedy oracle VOI policy (AUC 0.799 vs. 0.797), and with at most 0.5 questions per conversation it is 14.1 points more accurate than never asking. On real ABCD conversations one real exchange raises accuracy by 8.3 points where LAVOIR asks, on SGD the cap lowers the asking rate from 93% to 8.6%, and it answers a question in 31 ms median on a GH200.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Value of information (VOI) is a classic decision-theoretic quantity: the expected improvement in a decision from acquiring additional information before acting, often used to decide whether collecting more data is worth the cost. Single-pass decision encoders answer typed questions about a text in one forward pass with calibrated probabilities, but they cannot request missing information and instead guess when the first message is ambiguous. LAVOIR places candidate missing slots next to the answer options in the input, so the same forward pass that produces the decision also predicts the expected gain for each slot.

## [CRC-Router Adds Risk-Constrained Routing to Medical AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30714) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose CRC-Router, a risk-constrained, uncertainty-aware routing module that combines multiple uncertainty signals with predictive scores to decide when a medical AI system should act autonomously or escalate a case for human review. Instantiated on chest X-ray multi-finding triage with the NIH ChestX-ray14 dataset, it achieves the strongest empirical risk-coverage trade-off among evaluated baselines, both standalone and as a plug-in to the MedRAX agent, with code released on GitHub. Autonomous errors in medical AI can propagate into downstream clinical decisions, so a reliable escalation mechanism is a prerequisite for safe deployment. CRC-Router offers a model-agnostic way to enforce user-specified risk targets, which could accelerate the adoption of agentic AI in high-stakes clinical workflows while keeping clinicians in the loop. The method builds a per-finding routing feature vector from complementary uncertainty signals plus the predictive score, maps it to an estimated wrong-accept risk via a lightweight per-finding risk model, and then applies Conformal Risk Control to calibrate acceptance thresholds under a user-specified risk target. It is designed to work with both conventional medical prediction models and agentic medical AI systems, though evaluation is limited to chest X-ray triage on a single public dataset.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Conformal prediction is a framework that produces prediction sets with finite-sample coverage guarantees, and Conformal Risk Control extends it to control the expected value of any monotone loss function, such as the error rate of accepted predictions. Uncertainty quantification in medical AI aims to give confidence measures so that models can abstain or defer when inputs are ambiguous. Agentic AI systems in medical imaging are increasingly explored to improve throughput, but they raise the question of when autonomy is safe and when a case should be escalated to a human. CRC-Router sits at the intersection of these three lines of work.

## [Study Finds Cost-Inefficient Behaviors in Coding Agents, Skills Cut Costs 41.73%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30725) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30725v2) presents the first study of behavioral cost inefficiencies in coding agents, analyzing 1,200 trajectories from Claude Code and Mini-SWE-Agent across four configurations on SWE-bench Verified. It identifies three recurring cost-inefficient behaviors — subsumed retrieval, similar script generation, and test re-execution — and evaluates three mitigation strategies over 10k trajectories on held-out SWE-bench Verified and Pro tasks. As coding agents become widely deployed in software engineering workflows, their monetary costs are a growing concern, and this study shows that up to 22.75% of task cost comes from avoidable behaviors affecting 79%–98% of tasks. The finding that developer-designed skills reduce cost by up to 41.73% — roughly twice the gain from agent-synthesized skills — offers actionable guidance for teams optimizing agent cost and reliability. Structure-aware retrieval, one of the evaluated mitigations, can introduce retrieval overhead and alter agent delegation, causing inconsistent retrieval-efficiency improvements and cost increases of up to 28.14%. Agent-synthesized skills tend to produce low-level, trace-specific guidance that limits their effectiveness and generality, whereas developer-designed skills provide high-level, trace-agnostic guidance.

**Background**: Coding agents are LLM-based systems that autonomously analyze codebases, run commands, and patch code to fix real GitHub issues. ## [LW2S Learns to Skip Redundant Steps in Multi-Agent LLM Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30734) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30734) introduces Learning What to Skip (LW2S), a method that formulates component omission in multi-agent LLM workflows as counterfactual credit assignment. By learning action-specific safety models from controlled skip interventions and combining held-out calibration with domain-native guards, LW2S reduces recorded token cost while matching or improving full-workflow accuracy across mathematical reasoning, multiple-choice QA, and code generation with two instruction-model families. Multi-agent LLM workflows are increasingly used to boost task performance, but executing every planning, execution, verification, and summarization step wastes computation and can even overwrite correct intermediate answers. LW2S offers a principled way to cut token cost without sacrificing accuracy, which could make agentic pipelines cheaper and more practical for real-world deployment. The method learns from full-workflow logs that reveal the executed trajectory's reward, plus controlled skip interventions that reveal the consequences of omitting a future step; when an early skip is rejected, the controller can continue execution and reconsider a later component. The authors note that shared-error cases show agreement alone is insufficient for skip selection, and they run scale-up and second-topology experiments to examine component redundancy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent LLM workflows typically decompose a task into subtasks handled by specialized agents for planning, execution, verification, and summarization. Credit assignment in reinforcement learning is the problem of measuring an action's influence on future rewards, and counterfactual credit assignment estimates that influence by comparing what happened with what would have happened under a different action. LW2S applies this idea to decide which workflow components can be safely skipped.

## [ORCA: New Benchmark Tests LLMs on Data Science Code Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30749) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ORCA, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on Data Science Code Translation (DSCT), featuring 1,600 grounding-level tasks (ORCA-MAIN) across Data Querying, Data Manipulation, and Deep Learning, plus 200 project-level tasks (ORCA-PROJECT) spanning 7 data science task types. Each task includes annotated reference translations and test cases, and frontier models struggle: Claude-Opus-4.6 reaches only 56.92% success on ORCA-MAIN and 33.67% on ORCA-PROJECT. This fills a gap in LLM evaluation, which has focused heavily on data science code generation rather than translation between libraries, a task central to interoperability across data science ecosystems. The low success rates of frontier models highlight a concrete capability gap that could shape future model development and benchmarking priorities. The benchmark uses a multi-stage quality verification process to check task correctness and test-case robustness, and the authors observe a directional preference: translation is easier when source code uses more explicit, fine-grained operations. An intent-augmented method, where the model first infers source-code intent as extra context, yields average absolute gains of 4.80% on ORCA-MAIN and 5.33% on ORCA-PROJECT.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Data Science Code Translation (DSCT) means converting code from one data science library to another while preserving functional equivalence, so the translated code behaves the same as the original. Libraries such as pandas, NumPy, and PyTorch offer overlapping but non-identical APIs, making manual translation tedious and error-prone. LLMs have become popular tools for code generation, but their ability to translate code between these libraries has been under-studied, which is the gap ORCA aims to address.

## [HCOE maps BioBERT embeddings into hyperbolic space for clinical ontologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30763) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HCOE introduces a method that maps frozen BioBERT embeddings into a Poincaré ball, combining parent-side and child-side ontology-guided contrastive learning with coarse-to-fine ontology-path aggregation. Using ICD codes organized by CCS and ATC medication hierarchies, it achieves state-of-the-art results on ICD/ATC relation prediction, CCS-to-PheCode hierarchy transfer, and on MIMIC-IV for mortality, readmission, medication recommendation, and rare drug prediction. Biomedical language models capture textual semantics but do not explicitly preserve medical code hierarchies, which are central to clinical reasoning. By making clinical concept representations hierarchy-aware, HCOE could improve downstream clinical prediction and medication recommendation systems that rely on structured coding such as ICD and ATC. HCOE keeps BioBERT frozen and only learns the mapping into hyperbolic space, so the approach is a lightweight add-on rather than a full retraining of the language model. Evaluation is limited to ICD/ATC hierarchies and the MIMIC-IV dataset, so generalization to other coding systems or institutions remains to be tested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Hyperbolic embeddings place data in spaces of constant negative curvature, such as the Poincaré ball, whose volume grows exponentially and therefore represents tree-like hierarchies with less distortion than Euclidean space. BioBERT is a domain-specific language model adapted from BERT and pre-trained on large biomedical corpora, widely used for biomedical text mining. ICD is the International Classification of Diseases, and CCS (Clinical Classifications Software) groups ICD codes into clinically meaningful categories, while ATC provides a hierarchical classification of medications.

## [ConsultMind: Uncertainty-Aware Bayesian Framework for Automated Diagnostic Consultation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30796) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced AutoDisym, an automated pipeline that builds a Disorder-Symptom Bayesian Network (DSBN) by integrating diagnostic knowledge with heterogeneous diagnosis-labeled clinical narratives, and ConsultMind, an uncertainty-aware framework that updates disorder posteriors after each patient response and uses posterior uncertainty to guide inquiry and diagnosis. Evaluated across psychiatry, respiratory medicine, fever clinics, and three public datasets, AutoDisym achieved macro-averaged F1 scores of 81.37 for canonical symptoms and 72.19 for manifestations using GPT-5.6-Sol, while ConsultMind improved Top-1 and Top-3 diagnostic accuracy by up to 22.15 and 37.89 percentage points respectively. Automating diagnostic consultation is a long-standing goal in AI for healthcare, and this work shows that combining structured Bayesian reasoning with uncertainty-driven inquiry can substantially boost diagnostic accuracy while keeping decisions interpretable. If validated beyond preprint stage, such frameworks could support clinicians in resource-limited settings by guiding systematic history-taking and producing transparent differential diagnoses. The approach relies on a Disorder-Symptom Bayesian Network whose posteriors are updated after each response, with posterior uncertainty serving as the signal for whether to ask another question or commit to a diagnosis. Physician evaluation indicated that ConsultMind improved the quality of ranking explanations, differential diagnoses, and diagnosis rationales across LLMs of different scales, though the work remains an arXiv preprint without peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: A Bayesian network is a directed acyclic graph that encodes probabilistic dependencies between variables, allowing beliefs about diseases to be updated as new symptom evidence arrives. Diagnostic consultation is naturally a sequential decision-making process: clinicians ask questions one at a time, gather evidence, and stop when a diagnosis is sufficiently supported. This paper connects those two ideas by using the evolving uncertainty of disease posteriors to decide what to ask next and when to conclude.

## [Survey Unifies Fragmented Research on Real-Time Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30798) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv survey (2609.30798) synthesizes 38 primary sources across speech foundation modelling, turn-taking psycholinguistics, and agentic evaluation into an application-centric taxonomy of six categories. It proposes TRG (Timing-Recovery-Grounded), a reporting standard that characterizes agents by timing, post-disruption recovery, and state-verified outcomes, with a conditional fourth axis for multiparty deployments. Because architecture papers report latency, turn-taking papers report prediction accuracy, and agentic benchmarks report task success, no single number currently describes whether a deployed voice agent is actually good. This synthesis gives practitioners and researchers a shared vocabulary and evaluation framework for production voice agents, which have already moved from prototypes to real deployments. The paper makes three evidence-based claims: architecture choice is a deployment constraint rather than a settled verdict (a 2026 enterprise tutorial found no fully self-hostable end-to-end system meeting production constraints, while a chunked cascade independently reached state-of-the-art duplex behaviour); evaluation has shifted from component quality to grounded outcomes that verify backend state; and the dyadic assumption is breaking down, since multiparty turn-taking and multi-speaker reasoning require deciding when not to speak and who may be told what.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Real-time voice agents are systems that listen and speak in live conversation, and they are typically built either as end-to-end speech models or as cascaded pipelines chaining automatic speech recognition, machine translation or language modelling, and text-to-speech. Full-duplex behaviour means the agent can speak and listen simultaneously, supporting natural barge-in and backchannels, unlike half-duplex walkie-talkie-style turn-taking. Turn-taking psycholinguistics studies how humans predict when to speak, while agentic evaluation asks whether a system completed the user's task.

## [New Benchmark Exposes Epistemic Admission Failures in Shared Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30813) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Correlated Promotion Benchmark (CPB), which includes a static frozen test split (CPB-Static) and a live multi-agent evaluation (CPB-Live) to assess whether candidate claims should be admitted to shared agent memory. Evaluating eight admission policies across four agent families, it finds that deduplication-based policies reject many true claims while coverage-preserving policies admit nearly as many false claims as unrestricted sharing. This work highlights a fundamental trade-off in multi-agent memory systems: without access to source lineage, no non-oracle policy can consistently reject false claims across verbatim copies, paraphrases, and paraphrases declared authoritative. The findings have direct implications for AI safety and the design of trustworthy multi-agent coordination, as once an uncontested false belief enters memory, consumers assert it in 0.97–0.99 of probes. Gating on declared source type reduces false adoption to 0.06–0.09, compared with 0.22–0.47 for other answering policies, but no non-oracle policy consistently rejects false claims across all three claim types. The benchmark tracks source lineage defined by each scenario and uses a separate consumer that answers from the store alone.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Shared agent memory allows multiple AI agents to store and retrieve beliefs, but repeated claims can be mistaken for independent evidence, a problem known as correlated promotion. Epistemic admission refers to the policy decision of whether a candidate claim should be written into shared memory. This paper systematically evaluates such policies, revealing that deduplication and coverage-preserving approaches both have significant failure modes.

## [PTC-Decoder Boosts Small Language Models on Offline Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30836) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose PTC-Decoder (Plan-Tool Constrained Decoder), a training-free, plug-and-play decoder framework that combines a Plan-to-Act paradigm—elevating planning to an atomic tool forced at the first inference step—with TC-Decoder, a deterministic finite automaton imposing token-level hard constraints on tool names. Evaluated on 200 real remote-sensing satellite tasks across 7 SLMs, it achieves a statistically significant mean overall score gain of +1.21 (p<0.01, 95% CI [+1.13, +1.29]). This offers a lightweight way to improve step-level reliability of small language models on offline, resource-constrained edge devices such as remote sensing satellites, where limited reasoning capacity hinders multi-step tool orchestration. It could benefit edge AI and SLM deployment communities by enforcing plan adherence without retraining. An ablation study removing TC-Decoder causes substantial performance degradation across all quality metrics without reducing computational cost, confirming TC-Decoder as the primary driver. The authors note that final-answer accuracy remains an open challenge.

A deterministic finite automaton (DFA) is a computational model that processes input through a finite number of states and transitions, and here it is used to constrain the permissible output vocabulary during inference.

## [Energy Landscape Analysis Explains Jailbreaks in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30841) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30841) proposes interpreting safety alignment in diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs) as shaping a denoising energy landscape, where a well-aligned model routes harmful queries toward safe outputs through an energy barrier. From this framework, the authors derive three complementary, training-free detection signals—a step-0 ratio and two trajectory-velocity signals—and validate them across LLaDA-8B, LLaDA-1.5, Dream-7B, and LLaDA-MoE-7B. This work offers a unified theoretical lens for why jailbreak attacks succeed against diffusion language models, moving beyond ad hoc attack- and defense-specific analyses. The training-free detection signals could be directly integrated into dLLM safety pipelines, and the finding that detection and barrier-crossing thresholds are hard to separate has implications for how robust safety alignment can be in this emerging model class. The three signals are constructed so that an attack must either reveal its intent at initialization or expend kinetic energy to cross the barrier in at least one monitored subspace, meaning the signals cover each other's blind spots by construction. In stress tests of known attacks, every configuration that evaded detection also failed to produce harmful content, suggesting the detection and barrier-crossing thresholds are hard to separate.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) generate text through iterative parallel denoising rather than the left-to-right decoding of traditional autoregressive models, starting from a fully masked or noised sequence and refining it into coherent text. Safety alignment in such models is typically studied through specific attacks and defenses, but a shared framework explaining why attacks succeed has been lacking. Energy-based interpretations of denoising, where generation is viewed as minimizing an energy function, provide the conceptual basis for this paper's analysis.

## [SkillEvoReg: Regularizing Agent Skill Evolution Against Overfitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30861) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.30861) introduces SkillEvoReg, a general regularization framework for skill evolution in language-model agents that combines training-time skill dropout, complexity-aware local regularization, and causal counterexample validation (CCV). The authors instantiate it across SkillOpt, SkillEvolBench, and ContinualSkillBench, reporting that it consistently controls skill-state growth while preserving downstream capability and surfacing update-level regressions that structural metrics miss. As language-model agents increasingly convert execution experience into reusable external skills, repeated skill updates can accumulate redundant or task-specific instructions and disrupt previously working behavior — a problem the paper calls skill-evolution overfitting. SkillEvoReg shows that explicit regularization, borrowed from neural-network anti-overfitting practice, can complement increasingly capable skill updaters, which matters for anyone building long-running, self-improving agent systems. The framework retains each system's native skill evolver and task evaluator rather than replacing them, so it can be plugged into heterogeneous skill-evolution pipelines. Its three components target different failure modes: skill dropout perturbs update generation, complexity-aware local regularization limits unnecessary structural growth, and CCV provides targeted behavioral validation of candidate-specific regressions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Language-model agents are AI systems that use a large language model to plan and act on tasks, often by writing and reusing external "skills" such as code snippets or instruction templates. Overfitting is a classic machine-learning problem in which a model fits narrow training evidence so well that it generalizes poorly to new situations; this paper argues that repeated skill edits can suffer an analogous failure, where locally useful edits have persistent downstream effects that are not justified by the narrow evidence that produced them. Regularization refers to techniques that constrain a learning process to improve generalization, and causal counterexample validation is a validation approach that seeks counterexamples to test whether an observed behavior is genuinely caused by a specific change.

## [TISD: Trajectory-Intervention Self-Distillation for LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30878) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30878) introduces Trajectory-Intervention Self-Distillation (TISD), a branch-regenerate-distill algorithm that forces a teacher-selected branch action at the peak-divergence position of a failed rollout, lets the student regenerate the suffix, and then distills the full trajectory under a privileged-context-conditioned teacher. TISD improves average Avg@4 over SDPO by 1.2 percentage points on coding models and average Avg@128 by 0.8 points (equal-step) and 0.3 points (equal-time) across science domains. The work reframes teacher-student disagreement in on-policy self-distillation from a signal for local token repair into a signal for proposing trajectory branches, addressing a training-time data-collection bottleneck where OPSD cannot supervise successor contexts the student never samples. If validated, this could offer a more sample-efficient recipe for self-improvement in LLM post-training, relevant to teams working on reasoning, coding, and RL-style fine-tuning. The diagnostic framework uses controlled token interventions and finds that a teacher-preferred token at peak disagreement can improve student continuation success even though its local corrective value is limited. TISD only forces a single teacher-selected branch token before returning suffix generation to the student, and the abstract notes gains are measured under equal-step and equal-time budgets, with the paper's abstract truncated in the listing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) is a training strategy in which a model improves by learning from dense teacher targets evaluated along rollouts that the student itself samples, helping mitigate exposure bias. A key limitation is that when the teacher prefers a different action at a visited prefix, OPSD can only supervise that branch decision, not the successor contexts induced by the alternative action unless the student happens to sample them. TISD builds on this setting by using teacher-student disagreement to propose a trajectory branch rather than a local repair, then distilling the resulting full trajectory.

## [EXAONE Demand 1.0: A Foundation Model for Demand Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30880) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced EXAONE Demand 1.0, a time series foundation model (TSFM) built specifically for demand forecasting, combining a demand-specific corpus of 11.3M series and 48.4B observations from 73 sources with a synthetic generator, plus a demand-aware adapter that attaches low-rank branches to a frozen general-domain backbone. On 22 held-out datasets, both the real-plus-synthetic and synthetic-only versions outperformed 36 existing TSFMs, with real-world demand data adding further gains over synthetic data alone. Demand forecasting is central to supply chain, retail, and inventory planning, yet general-purpose TSFMs are pretrained on corpora where demand series are rare, so they handle short histories, frequent zeros, and stock-out censoring poorly. A model that explicitly targets these properties could give practitioners a stronger zero-shot or fine-tuning baseline, and the demand-class routing idea may influence how future domain-specific foundation models are designed. The adapter uses four low-rank branches, one for each demand class (smooth, intermittent, erratic, and lumpy), and a router that reads eight scale-free statistics of the input series to decide how much each branch contributes. The paper is an arXiv preprint (2609.30880v1) and has not yet undergone peer review, and the abstract does not report per-dataset error metrics or computational cost.

**Background**: Time series foundation models are pretrained on large, diverse collections of series and then applied to new forecasting tasks, often zero-shot, similar to how large language models generalize across text tasks. Demand forecasting is a specialized subfield where data often has short histories, many zero values, and censoring from stock-outs, and classical taxonomy splits demand into smooth, intermittent, erratic, and lumpy classes that require different modeling strategies. Low-rank adapters, popularized by LoRA, are lightweight modules added to a frozen pretrained model so it can be adapted to a new domain without retraining all parameters.

Researchers present MACBT, a clinician-facing AI decision-support system that encodes the five-stage CBT workflow (assessment, Socratic questioning, cognitive restructuring, behavioral experiments, treatment monitoring) into five collaborative agents, paired with a CBT-specific longitudinal memory module called CD Memory. By automating these tasks and tracking cognitive distortions across sessions, MACBT points toward AI decision support that could extend the reach of evidence-based psychotherapy without replacing the clinician. CD Memory tracks cognitive-distortion type, frequency, severity, and restructuring efficacy across sessions to generate pre-session pathology reports and intervention-priority recommendations, and the full memory-augmented system improves session quality by 12.6% and achieves a longitudinal mean of 2.29 on cross-session continuity, intervention progression, and personalization. ## [LLM Agents Show Financial Fragility Without Destabilizing Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30940) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces FRAIL, a controlled experimental framework that places LLM agents in three dynamic financial environments—bank runs, debt rollover, and reward crowdfunding—where each agent's decisions reshape the financial conditions faced by others. Testing seven leading LLMs, the authors find that 77% of baseline bank-run episodes and 83% of debt-rollover episodes end in collective failure even when no agent is instructed to destabilize the system, and they compare three stabilizing interaction mechanisms: compensated commitments, centralized commitment agreements, and participant-led coalitions. The findings suggest that individually capable LLM agents do not automatically form safe financial systems, meaning AI safety in finance must be evaluated at the system level rather than only per-agent. This has direct implications for financial AI governance, multi-agent system design, and the deployment of autonomous agents in markets where coordination failures can cascade. All three stabilizing mechanisms improved aggregate outcomes, but no single mechanism performed best across all financial structures; successful stabilization consistently shared a temporal pattern in which broad commitment formed early, before defensive behavior became self-reinforcing. The study is a simulation using stylized financial environments with no human subjects, personal data, or real financial transactions, and code is available at an anonymous repository.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: A bank run occurs when many depositors withdraw funds simultaneously out of fear of insolvency, potentially causing a solvent institution to fail; a debt rollover crisis occurs when borrowers cannot issue new debt to repay maturing debt. LLM agents are AI systems built on large language models that can autonomously make decisions and interact with other agents. Prior work on commitments in group interactions has studied how centralized versus personalized commitment arrangements influence cooperation, and this paper extends that question to LLM-driven financial systems.

A new arXiv paper (2609.30943) introduces LogicTree-RAG, a framework that induces a hierarchical logic tree as a global organizational backbone for retrieval-augmented generation, enabling automated long-form patent drafting without expert-defined outlines. Each logic tree node represents a technical element built through evidence-guided recursive generation, and a hybrid traversal mechanism maps the tree into balanced patent sections. Patent drafting is a knowledge-intensive, multi-expert task that existing LLM approaches handle only section-by-section or with manually crafted outlines, limiting scalable automation. By showing that logic-centric generation improves content quality, language conformity, and token efficiency over strong LLM baselines, this work points toward more autonomous AI assistance in legal-tech and other structured long-form technical writing. The logic tree is induced without expert-defined drafting priors, with high-level nodes capturing overarching concepts and deeper nodes elaborating technical details and implementation specifics. Experiments report consistently longer structured generation with high token efficiency, though the abstract does not quantify gains or detail evaluation benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

## [FTB Graph Maps First-Token Language Circuits in Multilingual LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30954) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces FTB Graph, an end-to-end structural circuit analysis of first-token language broadcasting across six model architectures from four families: GPT-2, BLOOM-560M, Pythia-1B/2.8B, and Qwen2.5-1.5B Base/Instruct. Using Edge Attribution Patching (EAP) with FP16 active clamping followed by exact activation patching verification with a 2,000-candidate-edge search ceiling, the authors extract directed acyclic graphs and find that Qwen2.5-1.5B base and instruct circuits retain 84.7% Jaccard similarity, including a Layer 27 hub. This work advances mechanistic interpretability by providing causal evidence for how multilingual LLMs decide their response language at the very first token, a decision that shapes all subsequent generation. It also shows that first-token routing is largely established during pretraining and preserved by instruction tuning, which could inform model design and multilingual alignment strategies. The evidence for deep or mid-to-deep broadcasting hubs is strongest for Pythia-2.8B and BLOOM-560M, while GPT-2 and Pythia-1B leave few out-of-graph heads for comparison and both Qwen2.5-1.5B variants invert the necessity check. Scaling from Pythia-1B to 2.8B expands node participation while maintaining a similar verified edge budget, producing sparser topology, and EAP scores correlate only weakly with exact patching deltas across most models, motivating exact-patching verification.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer the internal computations of neural networks into human-understandable circuits. Edge Attribution Patching (EAP) is a gradient-based method that uses a first-order Taylor approximation to score the importance of edges in a model's computational graph, while activation patching is a causal intervention technique that replaces activations from a clean run with those from a corrupted run to test necessity. Multilingual language models must resolve which language to respond in early during generation, but the causal circuitry behind this first-token language identity decision has remained poorly mapped.

## [MoMHa Optimizes LLM Harnesses for Accuracy, Safety, and Token Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30967) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Meta-Harness, a system that treats the Python code surrounding an LLM (the harness) as a first-class design surface and searches over three per-domain objectives: accuracy, behavioral safety, and token cost. Its central finding is that a single-phase joint-reward proposer, MoMHa, driven by the agentic coding tool Claude Code, outperforms a two-phase 'accuracy then tokens' ablation, scalar-only feedback, and an accuracy-only baseline across seventeen domains. Most LLM improvement work optimizes only accuracy, but this paper argues that an accurate harness which ignores unsafe requests or burns an order of magnitude more tokens is not actually a good harness. If harness strategies transfer to unseen benchmarks without retraining, as the paper reports for 8 of 12 target models, this could shift engineering effort from model fine-tuning toward automated optimization of the surrounding prompt, routing, and parsing code. MoMHa is evaluated on seventeen domains: seven synthetic capability suites, seven real-world public benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP, Spider, FEVER, MMLU-Pro, LawBench, NuminaMath), and three U-SafeBench-derived user-specific safety domains, using a 12-model fleet spanning four families. On the synthetic track it achieves a joint mean of 0.482 versus 0.198–0.422 for ten baselines (winning 7/10 columns), on the real-world track 0.461 versus 0.377 for the strongest baseline DSPy (winning 5/7 columns), and it attains the highest behavioral safety composite (0.781) while using 95 fewer tokens per example than the two-phase alternative.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: In production LLM systems, the model is only one component; a 'harness' is the surrounding Python code that constructs prompts, routes calls between models or tools, and parses outputs, and it is often what determines real-world reliability. Multi-objective optimization is a classical field concerned with problems where several goals conflict, typically characterized by a Pareto front of trade-off solutions rather than a single best answer. Claude Code is Anthropic's agentic command-line coding tool, which here acts as the proposer that reads prior harness source, execution traces, and scoring artifacts to suggest new harness variants.

Researchers released SciHorizon-eLab, an agentic protocol-to-task compiler that turns natural-language scientific protocols into executable, semantically grounded embodied tasks through semantic grounding, executable task synthesis, and multi-stage simulation-based certification. Using this pipeline they built a benchmark of 300 certified laboratory tasks, where the strongest policy achieves only a 49.7% average success rate. Automated scientific experimentation is bottlenecked by the lack of reliable, scalable evaluation environments, and this work replaces manual task engineering with a reproducible compilation pipeline. The compiler produces semantically grounded environments, executable manipulation programs, and step-level success specifications, and it supports human-in-the-loop task execution, reproducible expert-demonstration generation, and ordered step-level evaluation. The low 49.7% success rate of the best policy, along with observed weaknesses in human-agent coordination, highlights how far current embodied agents remain from reliable lab automation.

## [LLM Text Measures Vary Widely Across Models, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31013) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31013) evaluates thirteen LLM-based textual measures—including sentiment, management clarity, uncertainty, answer specificity, and climate and political risk—by having seven LLMs from different providers score earnings call transcripts of S&P 500 companies. Cross-model rank correlations average only 0.52, and transcript-level differences common across providers account for just 34% of total score variation. The findings undermine the common assumption that LLM-based textual measures are invariant to model choice, showing that coefficient magnitudes, signs, and statistical significance in downstream regressions vary substantially across models. This has direct implications for reproducibility and methodological validity in the growing body of social science and finance research that uses LLMs to convert corporate text into empirical variables. Cross-model disagreement does not predict subsequent analyst or market disagreement, suggesting a substantial model-specific component rather than common ambiguity in the underlying disclosure. Averaging across providers makes transcript rankings more stable for most constructs, but score levels remain sensitive to which models are included in the ensemble, so the authors recommend treating LLM-generated variables as model-contingent measurements validated across providers.

**Background**: In "text-as-data" research, scholars increasingly use generative LLMs to turn corporate disclosures such as earnings call transcripts into quantitative variables like sentiment or uncertainty scores, then feed those variables into regressions. This approach implicitly assumes that different LLMs would produce roughly the same numbers for the same text, an assumption the paper tests directly. Rank correlation is a standard statistic measuring how similar two rankings are, and it is used here to quantify agreement across models.

## [Governed Deduction: Policy-Grounded Premise Authorization Beyond Relevance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31029) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper formalizes Governed Deduction (GD), distinguishing premise authorization from relevance via a transition-local admission predicate admit(p, tau, S). Using an RBAC-augmented Spider benchmark, it constructs 4,461 matched authorization pairs and shows that a naive joint controller's 99.19% accuracy collapses to 50% after a role-name shortcut is removed by a frozen role permutation. This work highlights a critical gap in AI reasoning systems: they often conflate relevance with authorization, which can lead to unsafe or policy-violating inferences. The controlled negative finding and benchmark provide a foundation for evaluating policy-sensitive reasoning, impacting AI safety, access control, and formal methods research. The benchmark includes 4,461 matched authorization pairs and 1,856 held-out edges; after removing the role-name shortcut, premise/state-only, transition-only, and joint linear controllers all score exactly 50%, while a symbolic policy oracle remains at 100%. The paper emphasizes that matched one-sided controls and leakage audits are essential for evaluating learned policy-sensitive reasoning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Reasoning systems typically treat premise use as a relevance question: if a fact is available and useful, it may be selected for inference. Authorization imposes a different constraint: a premise may be represented and logically usable but not permitted for a particular local transition. Role-based access control (RBAC) is a standard method for regulating access based on user roles, and the Spider benchmark is a well-known text-to-SQL dataset for cross-domain databases.

## [Open-weight agents make LLM survey pollution harder to detect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31054) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31054) compares nine agent configurations, from fully open-weight local agents to closed commercial ones, each autonomously completing a survey with multiple response types and detection checks. Fully open agents ran locally at no usage cost and performed competitively with commercial alternatives, while open and commercial agents failed different sets of checks and no single check reliably detected all of them. This identifies cheap, open-weight agents as a distinct and harder-to-mitigate source of LLM pollution, threatening the validity of survey and online behavioral research that assumes responses come from humans. It also suggests that detection strategies must become multilayered, with open-text analysis as the most promising discriminator. The study evaluated nine agent configurations on a survey containing multiple response types and various detection checks, finding that open-text responses discriminated best between agents and humans. A key caveat is that no single check caught every agent, so detection depends on combining signals rather than relying on one test.

**Background**: LLM pollution refers to synthetic model-generated responses contaminating datasets meant to capture genuine human behavior, which can distort empirical findings and obscure the origin of data. Open-weight models are AI models whose learned parameters are publicly released, allowing anyone to download and run them locally, often under permissive licenses. Agentic frameworks are software toolkits that let an LLM autonomously plan, use tools, and complete multi-step tasks such as filling out a survey.

## [SCALPEL: Contrastive Sparse Autoencoders for Selective Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31056) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SCALPEL, a contrastive sparse autoencoder that learns more selective forget features for representation-level machine unlearning. It identifies an energy bias in reconstruction-based extraction that favors dominant background structure over low-energy target-specific components, and validates the method on TOFU across Qwen, Llama, and Gemma, substantially improving over NMF and standard SAE interventions and being competitive with Gradient Difference and RMU. This work bridges mechanistic interpretability and fine-grained unlearning, addressing practical privacy needs such as GDPR requests where only a narrow target, like one person's information, must be removed. It could affect researchers in AI safety, privacy, and interpretability by offering a more selective representation-level intervention. The authors show theoretically that contrastive training promotes target-selective features and that their selection score controls expected background knowledge perturbation. The method is evaluated on TOFU, a benchmark of fictitious author facts, and compared against NMF, standard SAE interventions, Gradient Difference, and RMU.

**Background**: Machine unlearning aims to remove targeted information from a model while preserving its other abilities, and behavioral forgetting alone may be insufficient, motivating interventions on internal representations. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are a central tool in mechanistic interpretability that decompose dense language-model activations into sparse, often interpretable features. TOFU is a benchmark of fictitious unlearning that tests whether a model behaves as if it never learned the forgotten data. Contrastive learning trains a model to distinguish similar and dissimilar examples, which here helps isolate target-specific features from background structure.

## [Externalized CPDAG Summaries Boost LLM Causal Deduction by 13.4 Points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31071) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Structured Thinking, a two-turn pipeline that first externalizes a typed, schema-constrained CPDAG summary and then answers causal queries against that graph state. On the Corr2Cause full test, it raises Qwen3.5-27B from 73.0 to 86.4 F1(Yes) over a strong PC-instruction baseline, a +13.4 pp gain (McNemar p=2.4e-6; bootstrap 95% CI [+8.4, +18.6]). The result suggests a general design principle for LLM reasoning: externalize the latent object that defines the label and constrain its form, rather than relying on free-form chain-of-thought. This could improve reliability of LLMs on structured causal and scientific reasoning tasks, and the same pattern held on Qwen3.6-27B, Paraphrase-OOD, and GPT-5.4-mini. A PC-scaffolded two-turn prose control reaches only 67.6 F1, showing that a detailed PC scaffold plus a schema-free prose intermediate is insufficient; scrambling the emitted CPDAG costs 12.0 pp F1, and a full-split audit shows close agreement with the reference CPDAG (ID skeleton F1 0.960; exact match 75.9%). Across three full-ID seeds the mean gain is +8.1 ± 5.3 pp, indicating some seed variance.

**Background**: Corr2Cause asks whether a causal claim holds in every DAG compatible with observed correlations and conditional independencies. The PC algorithm is a standard constraint-based method for causal discovery that outputs such a CPDAG, and free-form chain-of-thought often collapses this equivalence-class problem into local pattern matching.

## [LIFT Transfers LLM Reasoning Vectors to Boost VLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31140) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31140) introduces LIFT (Language-side reasonIng Facilitation and Transfer), a lightweight vector-intervention method that transfers reasoning capability from a base LLM to an aligned vision-language model without retraining the backbone. LIFT defines Reasoning Vectors as answer-token hidden-state differences between a Reasoner path with an explicit reasoning trace and a Solver path without it, then injects these vectors into the language-side activations of the target VLM. Most VLMs are built by extending pretrained LLMs with visual modules, but this multimodal scaling often degrades the language-side reasoning originally encoded in the base LLM. LIFT shows that the base LLM is a more effective source for recovering this lost reasoning than the aligned VLM itself, offering a lightweight, retraining-free path to improve multimodal reasoning. Evaluated on two VLMs across six reasoning benchmarks under matched protocols, LLM-derived Reasoning Vectors consistently outperformed VLM-derived vectors, and further analysis showed the vectors influence intermediate reasoning behavior rather than merely altering final answers. The method supports learnable vector adaptation while keeping the VLM backbone frozen, and source code is promised soon.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) typically combine a pretrained Large Language Model (LLM) with visual encoders and multimodal alignment training so the model can process images and text together. Prior work has observed that this alignment process can erode the language reasoning skills the base LLM once had, even though those skills remain latent in the base model. Vector intervention is an inference-time technique that modifies hidden activations to steer model behavior without changing weights, and LIFT applies this idea to transfer reasoning signals across models.

## [SemNav Boosts Repository-Level Issue Localization with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31176) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SemNav is a new framework that combines deterministic retrieval with an LLM agent and a Semantic Navigation Graph to improve repository-level issue localization. On SWE-bench Lite and PLocBench, it raises File Hit@10 from 68.33% to 82.67% with Gemma 4B, and improves downstream issue resolution from 44.00% to 52.33%. Issue localization is a critical first step in AI-assisted software engineering, and better localization directly improves an agent's ability to resolve real bugs. SemNav's gains suggest that structured navigation and evidence tracking can make LLM coding agents more reliable on large repositories. SemNav uses three components: a Semantic Navigation Graph that resolves program relations on demand via a language server, Issue-conditioned Semantic Cards that give compact source-grounded entity interpretations, and a persistent Candidate Workspace that records each candidate with its evidential basis. Semantic Cards reduce working-context load by 48.2% compared with reading full source files, and SemNav ranks first on all seven evidence-quality metrics on SWE-Explore.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Repository-level issue localization means identifying and ranking the files and functions relevant to fixing a reported issue. LLM agents typically do this iteratively, proposing candidate locations, inspecting code, and revising judgments as new evidence appears, but existing environments offer limited support for this loop. SemNav addresses this by seeding a broad candidate set with deterministic retrieval and then refining it with an LLM agent guided by structured evidence.

## [SPO: LLM-Driven Stackelberg Program Optimization for Adaptive LNS Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31179) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Stackelberg Program Optimization (SPO), an LLM-based framework that discovers adaptive executable destroy-repair programs for Large Neighborhood Search by conditioning operator decisions on a compact LNS state. Experiments on the traveling salesperson problem (TSP) and capacitated vehicle routing problem (CVRP) show SPO outperforms strong baselines across a broad range of settings and generalizes to larger instances and benchmark sets. This work addresses a core combinatorial optimization challenge by automating the design of LNS operators, which traditionally require substantial human expertise and manual tuning. It could benefit researchers in AI for optimization and program synthesis by showing how LLM-driven program discovery can be combined with game-theoretic credit assignment to produce state-dependent, coupled operator behavior. SPO organizes destroy-repair discovery as a Stackelberg interaction over program space, treating destroy programs as leaders and repair programs as conditional follower responses, with role-specific credits guiding a coupled optimization that combines LLM generator learning with population-based evolutionary search. Behavioral analyses demonstrate state-dependent operator behavior and coupled destroy-repair improvement during discovery.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Large Neighborhood Search (LNS) is a metaheuristic for combinatorial optimization that iteratively destroys part of a solution and repairs it, and its performance depends heavily on the destroy and repair operators used. Adaptive LNS (ALNS) dynamically selects among operators, but designing effective operators still typically relies on human expertise. Stackelberg games model asymmetric leader-follower interactions, which SPO uses to capture the asymmetric dependency between destroy and repair roles.
