## [Latent Variable Framework Corrects Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31184) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31184) proposes a unified latent variable framework that jointly models pairwise and ordinal comparison data while explicitly correcting for documented LLM judge biases such as position bias, verbosity bias, judge severity, and self-enhancement. The authors claim this approach recovers reliable model rankings from substantially fewer comparisons than current leaderboards, which compensate for bias by simply running more evaluations. LLM-as-a-judge has become the de facto standard for scalable subjective evaluation, so systematic judge bias directly undermines the trustworthiness of widely cited leaderboards. Because fitting the proposed model costs negligible compute relative to a single round of LLM inference, bias correction could make evaluation both more statistically rigorous and far more computationally sustainable. The framework handles both pairwise and ordinal data in a single measurement model, treating judges as biased instruments rather than neutral ones. The paper argues that no volume of additional comparisons can eliminate these confounders, since the root cause is an incomplete measurement model rather than insufficient data.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Latent variable models are statistical models that relate observed data (manifest variables) to unobserved factors (latent variables), and are widely used in psychometrics and natural language processing. In LLM evaluation, a common setup asks a strong model to judge which of two candidate responses is better, producing pairwise comparison data that is then aggregated into leaderboard rankings. Documented judge biases include preferring the first-listed response (position bias), favoring longer answers (verbosity bias), and favoring outputs from the same model family (self-enhancement).

## [New Paper Proposes 'Evolutionary Safety' Framework for Recursive Self-Improving AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31186) ⭐️ 7.0/10

An arXiv paper (2609.31186v1) introduces 'Evolutionary Safety,' a framework for studying how safety properties change, persist, accumulate, and propagate in recursively self-improving AI systems. It characterizes recurring failure modes such as intent drift, error accumulation, experience contamination, safety-property erosion, evaluator drift, and risk propagation, and develops a taxonomy spanning persistent agent state, model state, evaluation and environmental feedback, computational substrate, and meta-level update mechanisms. As AI systems increasingly participate in their own training, agent evolution, and automated development, traditional point-in-time safety evaluations may become insufficient, making this framework relevant to AI safety researchers, alignment teams, and policymakers. The paper also derives governance principles for modification, selection, authorization, provenance, and recovery, offering structure to an under-explored area at the intersection of AI safety and recursive self-improvement. The taxonomy organizes evolutionary risks across states, updates, trajectories, and lineages, and the authors outline open problems toward maintaining safety guarantees as systems become more persistent, adaptive, and recursively self-improving. Project resources and proposed evaluation systems are available at the linked project page, though the abstract is truncated and no empirical results are described in the provided content.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) refers to a hypothesized process in which an AI system improves its own ability to improve, potentially leading to rapid capability gains; no current system has shown signs of such an intelligence explosion. AI alignment is the subfield of AI safety concerned with steering AI systems toward intended goals and preventing misaligned behavior such as reward hacking or power-seeking. This paper sits at the intersection of these areas, arguing that safety must be studied not just at a single moment but across an AI system's ongoing evolutionary process.

## [Data Scaling Decomposed into Samples, Sources, and Spatial Coverage for Brain Microarchitecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31201) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper studies data scaling as an allocation problem by separating unique sample count, source diversity, and spatial coverage, rather than treating training data as a single count. Across 93 controlled pretraining runs of a contrastive model on 11.6 million spatially anchored image patches from 21 human brains, performance improved with more unique samples, broader spatial coverage, additional compute, and larger model capacity, but distributing a fixed sample budget across more subjects produced no detectable improvement. This work shows that for hierarchically and spatially structured data, simply adding more sources does not help when the sample budget is fixed, which challenges the common assumption that more diverse data sources always improve representation learning. The findings are relevant to medical imaging, histology, and other spatial domains where data collection is expensive and allocation decisions matter. The study used a contrastive model supervised by spatial proximity, with a source defined as an individual brain and a sample as an image patch at a specific spatial location. At a fixed sample count, distributing samples across one to 18 subjects produced no detectable improvement, even though representations generalized substantially better to subjects seen during pretraining, indicating that inter-subject variation strongly affects generalization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Scaling studies in machine learning typically describe training data with a single count of samples, but this ignores how data is distributed across sources and space. In whole-brain histology, data is naturally hierarchical and spatial: each brain is a source, and each image patch sits at a specific location. Contrastive learning is a self-supervised approach that learns representations by pulling similar samples together and pushing dissimilar ones apart, and here spatial proximity is used to define similarity.

## [Influence Estimators Disagree Due to Specification Mismatch, Not Approximation Error](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31214) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31214) argues that incompatible rankings produced by different influence estimators stem primarily from specification mismatch rather than approximation error. It formalizes influence as a counterfactual estimand and organizes representative estimators by their implied specifications, deriving a local decomposition of behavior signals, training signals, and counterfactual parameter responses. This reframing could change how researchers interpret and compare data attribution methods used for data debugging, valuation, and attribution, since disagreements previously blamed on approximation error may actually reflect different target quantities. It matters for anyone relying on influence functions to identify mislabeled data or attribute LLM outputs. The paper distinguishes specification mismatch across estimands from approximation error in estimating a fixed estimand, and shows in controlled experiments that exact estimands under different specifications can induce different rankings, while approximation error grows as perturbations move farther from their linearization points. Experiments on noisy label detection and LLM attribution show that the choice of behavior surrogate—such as query loss, a logit, or a margin—significantly affects attribution quality.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Influence functions originated in robust statistics in the 1970s and 1980s to measure how sensitive an estimator is to small changes in data, and were adapted to machine learning in a 2017 paper. They approximate the sensitivity of model predictions to training examples locally around the final weights, which can miss nonlinear training phenomena. Data attribution uses such methods to clarify the connection between training data and model outcomes for transparency and accountability.

## [DIAL Framework Debias LLM Judges and Calibrates to Human Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31215) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced DIAL, a unified framework that combines abundant LLM comparisons with limited human comparisons to separate judge-specific position effects, learn shared structure in position-debiased LLM preferences, and adaptively calibrate that structure toward human preferences. The paper provides theoretical analysis of identification, adaptive estimation, and uncertainty quantification, and validates the approach with over 410K judgments from 21 LLM judges in both display orders across three human-preference benchmarks. LLM-as-a-judge is increasingly used for scalable evaluation, but position bias and systematic divergence from human preferences undermine its reliability. DIAL addresses both issues simultaneously, potentially enabling more trustworthy automated evaluation with fewer human labels, which could benefit AI evaluation, alignment, and model development workflows. The framework separates judge-specific position effects from shared preference structure and uses adaptive estimation to balance LLM anchoring against limited human evidence, with fixed-weight uncertainty quantification for the calibrated human preference. Empirical results show robustness to unbalanced response order and strong human-aligned rankings with limited labels, and the authors release a dataset of over 410K judgments from 21 LLM judges in both display orders.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Position bias in LLM judges refers to the tendency to prefer responses based on their ordinal position rather than quality, and it is commonly measured by swap consistency and mitigated by answer swapping, which doubles evaluation cost. Even after removing position effects, LLM judgments can still diverge systematically from human preferences, motivating calibration methods that align automated evaluations with human judgment. DIAL builds on these ideas by jointly modeling position effects and human preference calibration in a multi-judge setting.

## [MA-WAM: Multi-Agent World-Action Model for Test-Time Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31281) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.31281) introduces MA-WAM, a test-time planning framework that lets a frozen multi-agent flow policy evaluate candidate joint actions by predicting their consequences through cross-agent dependencies. The authors claim it is the first test-time world-model planner for multi-agent flow policies, and across 30 offline MARL settings on MAMuJoCo, SMAC, and MPE it achieves mean relative gains of 22.0% over direct execution and 25.6% over uniform action selection. Multi-agent coordination is a core challenge in robotics and embodied AI, and this work shows that test-time planning with a world model can substantially improve team performance without retraining the underlying policy. Because the planner adds only 12.1 ms (2.5% of measured generation-and-scoring time) on an A100 GPU, the approach is practical for time-sensitive multi-agent control. MA-WAM explicitly models cross-agent dependencies when scoring joint actions, unlike a naive extension that applies a single-agent world model to each agent's action independently. The planner operates on a frozen multi-agent flow policy, proposing fixed-length joint-action sequences from the current observation context and scoring them by predicted cumulative return.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: World models are learned simulators that predict future observations and rewards, allowing an agent to plan by imagining the outcomes of candidate actions before executing them. Flow policies are a class of generative action models that produce actions through an iterative flow-based generation process, and multi-agent versions must coordinate several such policies acting simultaneously. In multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL), each agent's action affects both the observations and the responses of teammates and opponents, so predicting team return requires reasoning about the joint action rather than treating agents independently.

## [Small Local Models: Accuracy-Energy Trade-offs in Document Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31341) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31341) benchmarks small (≤8B parameter) text-only and vision-language models for on-premise information extraction, evaluated on both accuracy and energy across input representation, model family, and inference configuration. It finds that batching is the dominant energy lever, cutting energy per page by 38-85% at no accuracy cost, while FP8 quantization saves 27-32% only when requests are served one at a time. For organizations handling privacy-sensitive documents on-premise, this provides concrete, actionable guidance on which pipeline to choose and how to cut energy use without sacrificing accuracy. It also shows that the best representation is document-dependent, so a single universal pipeline is not optimal. On near-plain-text Kleister-NDA contracts, small text-only models with a cheap parser are both more accurate and cheaper than any vision-language configuration, while vision-language models win on layout-rich VRDU forms. Preprocessing dominates remaining energy costs: neural OCR consumes 17× more energy per page than classical OCR and never reaches the Pareto frontier.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Information extraction pipelines can either feed parsed text or page images to a model; vision-language models (VLMs) jointly interpret images and text, extending text-only LLMs to multimodal input. FP8 quantization stores model weights and activations in 8-bit floating point to reduce memory and compute, while batching groups multiple requests into one inference pass to improve hardware utilization. Kleister-NDA and VRDU are public benchmarks for key information extraction from long, formally structured documents.

## [Mutable Transcripts Let Users Edit Chat History to Fight Context Pollution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31354) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31354) by Dan Barry and Andrew Hines introduces "mutable transcripts," an interaction paradigm in which users can revise prior conversation turns via natural-language edit requests instead of only appending new messages. The authors built a working prototype (open-sourced as ReChat) and evaluated it in a controlled user study with 17 participants, who significantly preferred it over standard chat for clarity, confidence, and ease of use. Context pollution is a real and growing pain point in LLM chat: outdated or corrected information stays in the model's working context and keeps influencing later responses, often forcing users to restart conversations. By reframing the transcript as editable conversational state rather than an immutable log, this work could influence how future chat interfaces and agent frameworks manage memory and user intent. The prototype integrates transcript-level revision directly into a standard chat interface, and transcript analysis of study conversations showed that mutable transcripts can shorten conversations and eliminate obsolete retained context. However, the evaluation is limited to a small controlled study (n=17) and illustrative scenario analysis, so broader scalability, model-side implementation, and long-term effects remain untested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Most LLM chat systems treat conversation history as an immutable sequence of turns that forms the model's working context, and the model itself is stateless between calls—every response is generated from the accumulated transcript. Because real user intent evolves through corrections, refinements, and shifting constraints, this static history can accumulate irrelevant or outdated content, a problem known as context pollution. Related efforts include branching conversations, memory trees, and context-management tools that let users delete or steer prior messages, but mutable transcripts specifically allow natural-language edits to rewrite the history itself.

## [Study Reconstructs PoLar Dynamic Layer Routing, Fails to Replicate Learned Router](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31360) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper reconstructs and extends the program-of-layers (PoLar) approach, which dynamically routes LLM inputs through adaptive sequences of skipped or repeated contiguous layer blocks instead of a fixed forward pass. Across 5 models, the authors reproduced PoLar's core findings—skipping beats the standard pass, repeating beats skipping, and combining both beats either alone—but failed to replicate the main claim about the learned router for single-shot inference, whose top-ranked prediction consistently collapsed back to the standard pass. This work matters because dynamic layer routing could make LLM inference more efficient by spending less compute on easy inputs and more on hard ones, echoing how the brain routes information via the thalamus. The failure to replicate the learned router also highlights reproducibility concerns in a fast-growing area of efficient transformer research. The authors found that a small number of generic programs suffice to solve most questions, and that error-correcting programs are highly brittle—undoing even a single edit inside a program typically breaks the correction. Shorter programs sufficed for easier questions, while harder questions required more layer repeats, and the code is publicly released.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Standard LLM inference runs every input through all layers in a fixed order, regardless of difficulty. PoLar, introduced by Li et al. (2026), treats a frozen pretrained LLM's layers as a library of atomic functions, letting each input get its own "program" that skips, keeps, or repeats layer segments. This paper uses Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS), a search algorithm that explores decision trees, to diagnose which layer configurations work, drawing an analogy to thalamo-cortical coordination in the brain.

## [Anchored Context Distillation Compresses Tool Observations in SE Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31430) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The arXiv paper introduces Latent Observations, Hard Actions (LOHA), a context layout that compresses older tool observations into soft tokens while keeping the agent's own turns and the last K observations in text, combined with Anchored Context Distillation (ACD), a training method that lets the agent read latent tokens while constraining behavioral drift. On SWE-bench Verified with K=3, context per call drops 43% for Qwen3-4B and 57% for SWE-Master-4B-RL, with resolve rates of 12.1% and 21.8% versus 14.5% and 27.5% for uncompressed bases. Long interaction histories are a core bottleneck for LLM-based software engineering agents, and this work shows that compressing only tool observations—not the agent's own actions—can cut context substantially while preserving much of the agent's behavior. If the approach generalizes, it could enable longer-horizon coding agents and higher serving throughput under fixed token limits. The method keeps the agent's own turns and the last K observations in plain text for exact reference while compressing older observations into soft tokens, and ACD distills the base model's full-text predictions into the latent view while anchoring behavior on plain-text inputs. Under a 32K-token limit, Qwen3 with K=3 resolves 21.1% of a 199-instance subset versus 11.1% for the same adapted agent using full text, and in concurrent single-GPU serving it achieves 1.9x that full-text agent's instance throughput; a recency sweep reaches 14.4% and 23.0% at K=8, with larger windows generally favoring task performance over compression.

Soft-token context compression encodes text into shorter embedding sequences that a model can read directly, but naively adapting an agent to such representations can change its behavior and discard information needed for later actions. Knowledge distillation trains a model to match a stronger teacher's outputs, and here it is used to teach the agent to read compressed latent observations while staying anchored to its original full-text behavior.

## [UQ-LOB Adds Calibrated Uncertainty to Limit Order Book Forecasts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31491) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced UQ-LOB, a lightweight, encoder-agnostic uncertainty quantification module that attaches to any pretrained limit order book (LOB) encoder and, inspired by attentive neural processes, conditions each forecast on a context set of recently completed windows. It offers a UQ-regression variant producing a calibrated Gaussian over future tick displacement and a UQ-classification variant producing a categorical distribution over down/up/stationary, both exposing a scalar confidence for selective prediction. Most deep LOB forecasters are point predictors that never indicate which forecasts can be trusted, so this work addresses a key gap in trustworthy deep learning for finance. By enabling selective prediction, it could let algorithmic trading systems abstain on low-confidence signals and act only on high-confidence ones, improving risk control in high-frequency trading. Evaluated on 5.2 billion LOB events across seven cryptocurrency assets and horizons of 5, 10 and 15 seconds, UQ-regression achieves near-nominal 68% interval coverage, and restricting to the most confident 10% of predictions raises directional macro F1 by 0.11-0.15 for UQ-regression and 0.05-0.11 for UQ-classification at every horizon. On large, economically meaningful moves, the tightest confidence tier reaches directional F1 of 0.88 (down) and 0.83 (up) at the 5-second horizon.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: A limit order book records all outstanding buy and sell orders for an asset, and short-horizon mid-price movement forecasting from this data is central to algorithmic trading. Uncertainty quantification (UQ) in deep learning aims to tell a model when it is likely to be wrong, often by producing calibrated probability distributions rather than single point estimates. Attentive neural processes are meta-learning models that use attention to condition predictions on a set of observed context points, yielding calibrated uncertainty, and UQ-LOB adapts this idea to LOB forecasting.

## [Flow Matching Unifies Neural Representational Dissimilarity Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31544) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31544) shows that a variety of neural representational dissimilarity metrics can be unified under the flow matching framework from deep generative models, arising as Jeffreys divergences under different velocity constraints. The framework also enables principled estimation of distances for complicated distributions and the design of new metrics. This unification bridges deep generative modeling and computational neuroscience, offering a single principled approach to compare neural codes across stimuli, brain areas, tasks, and models. It could simplify method selection and inspire new dissimilarity metrics for researchers in both machine learning and neuroscience. The key technical insight is that commonly used distance metrics, which previously relied on different assumptions and separate estimation methods, emerge as Jeffreys divergences under different velocity constraints within flow matching. The authors report that flow matching is particularly advantageous for estimating distances involving complicated distributions and continuous variables.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Flow matching is a simulation-free, ODE-based generative modeling framework that learns time-dependent velocity fields to transform noise into data distributions, introduced by Lipman et al. in 2022. Neural representational dissimilarity quantifies differences between neural response distributions, often summarized via representational dissimilarity matrices (RDMs). Jeffreys divergence is a symmetric statistical divergence that combines the two directions of the Kullback-Leibler divergence, measuring how different two probability distributions are.

## [Steiner's Taxonomy Reveals How Multi-Agent LLM Teams Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31563) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Steiner's taxonomy of group tasks as a framework for analyzing multi-agent LLM scaling, testing 13 open-weight models with teams of up to 30 agents. It finds that on disjunctive tasks larger teams raise the chance of a correct answer by 5-20 points, but plurality voting captures almost none of this benefit, while on compensatory Fermi estimation tasks scaling yields only about a 6% error reduction. The results challenge the common assumption that simply adding more agents improves multi-agent LLM systems, showing that task structure and the output-combining mechanism jointly determine whether scaling helps. This has direct implications for how practitioners design voting, aggregation, and revision pipelines in multi-agent deployments. The paper models independently sampled agents as conditionally independent given the item, so plurality voting converges to the model's modal answer and averaging converges to its item-level bias. Multi-round revision substantially raises accuracy, but the gain is nearly identical with one peer as with 29, and combining model families helps on Fermi estimation yet does not surpass the strongest member on disjunctive tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Steiner's taxonomy, from Ivan Dale Steiner's book Group Processes and Productivity, classifies group tasks by how member contributions combine, and is used to explain coordination losses in groups. Disjunctive tasks are completed when a single member's solution is adopted, whereas compensatory tasks let stronger contributions offset weaker ones through aggregation. Multi-agent LLM systems typically combine outputs via plurality voting or averaging, and this paper asks how those mechanisms interact with task type as team size grows.

Researchers released ENAS, an open-source hardware-aware Neural Architecture Search framework that operates without GPUs and achieves mean search-time speedups of 2.41x and 1.70x on the Visual Wake Words and Melanoma Cancer benchmarks, respectively, compared with the recent NanoNAS framework. On an STM32H743 microcontroller, ENAS reached 79.4% test accuracy, outperforming a greedy CPU-only baseline by 2.6 percentage points. By removing the GPU requirement, ENAS makes hardware-aware NAS accessible to developers and labs with limited compute resources, which is important for the fast-growing TinyML ecosystem where models must fit within kilobytes of SRAM. Its lower peak activation RAM directly addresses the binding memory constraint for microcontroller deployment, potentially enabling more accurate models on the same tiny devices. ENAS combines a static feasibility check, a cell-based search space supporting standard, depthwise-separable, and bottleneck blocks with optional skip connections, and a three-stage hybrid search strategy (random, then top-K, then mutation) with persistent cross-run caching. It was evaluated on eight microcontrollers with 20 KB to 1 MB SRAM across nine input image resolutions, and the framework is released as open source on GitHub.

## [Sparse Neurons in Frozen BERT Drive AI-Text Detection, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30287) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30287) applies L1-to-L2 sparse probing to all 9,216 CLS hidden-state dimensions of frozen BERT-base-uncased, identifying a stable set of under 1% of neurons per generator that retain most AI-text detection accuracy on the RAID benchmark. Bidirectional activation patching confirms these neurons are causally relevant, flipping predictions an order of magnitude more often than random sets, while mean-ablation leaves accuracy largely intact. This mechanistic interpretability analysis could inform both the robustness of AI-text detectors and our understanding of how BERT encodes authorship signals, potentially enabling more efficient detectors that operate on a small fixed subspace. It also links detection behavior to post-training alignment, showing instruction-tuned generators leave a distinct layer-12 footprint. Cross-generator analysis reveals a bipartite structure: instruction-tuned generators concentrate 30-36% of stable neurons in BERT's final layer, while base generators fall below 14%. Leave-one-family-out evaluation shows the selected neurons retain 86-94% of the full-feature ceiling on unseen generator families, meaning a detector can work without re-identifying neurons per generator.

**Background**: Sparse probing is a mechanistic interpretability technique that trains sparse linear classifiers on individual neuron activations to find which neurons encode a given feature. Activation patching (also called causal tracing) swaps internal activations between inputs to test whether specific components causally affect a model's output. RAID is the largest benchmark for AI-generated text detection, spanning over 10 million documents, 11 LLMs, 11 genres, and 12 adversarial attacks. BERT-base-uncased is a widely used 12-layer, 768-dimensional transformer encoder whose CLS token aggregates sequence-level representations.

## [Cartograph: Federated MCP Proxy Cuts Agent Tool Discovery to O(k)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30293) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Cartograph is a federated MCP proxy that replaces full-catalog tool loading with operator-attested capability cards and two-stage retrieval, exposing only three proxy tools instead of 374 definitions on a 22-server deployment. On a 49-query benchmark it achieves R@5 of 0.816 versus 0.592 for a Jaccard keyword baseline, while a top-5 discovery exchange uses 475 tokens instead of 42,450. As MCP tool catalogs grow, loading every definition becomes expensive and degrades agent context, so Cartograph's O(k) progressive disclosure directly addresses a scalability bottleneck for agent tool use. Its operator-attested provenance also matters for trust and security, since ranking is based on signed descriptions controlled by the deploying operator rather than publisher-supplied copy. Cartograph combines Ed25519-signed capability cards, a three-layer confusable-cluster analysis called Rift, and server-before-tool ranking; Rift found 49 confusable clusters, including four HIGH-risk ones in bootstrap-generated cards. Gateway measurements over ten trials add only 5ms mean latency (0.8%) versus direct stdio MCP calls, though an exploratory comparison of 119 LLM-generated descriptions showed that mixing card-generation regimes can reduce R@5.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard that lets AI applications connect to external data sources and tools, but agents typically must load tool definitions before using them. Two-stage retrieval is a common information-retrieval pattern that first retrieves a coarse candidate set and then reranks it more precisely. Ed25519 is a high-speed elliptic-curve digital signature algorithm used here to attest that capability cards come from the deploying operator.

## [SlideLab: Training-Free Multi-Agent Framework for Scientific Slides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30294) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SlideLab is a training-free multi-agent framework that generates scientific presentations from research papers by first planning a narrative and then building and iteratively refining a shared slide deck using agents for content planning, visual generation, layout refinement, and grounding verification. In a blind human preference study, it was preferred over both open-source and commercial systems on 77% of papers while using roughly 4 times fewer inference tokens than the strongest open-source baseline, and the paper also introduces ConfArena, an audience-oriented evaluation framework that simulates a conference room and assesses presentations slide by slide. This work matters because it shows that a training-free, multi-agent pipeline can beat both open-source and commercial presentation-generation systems on human preference while being far more token-efficient, which lowers the cost and barrier for researchers who need to turn papers into talks. ConfArena also offers a new audience-oriented evaluation paradigm that matches human system rankings and can detect injected problems such as falsified numbers, degraded figures, dropped slides, and shuffled slide order, addressing a gap in how presentation generation is measured. SlideLab is training-free, meaning it relies on prompting and coordination among agents rather than fine-tuning a model, and its pipeline includes narrative planning, content planning, visual generation, layout refinement, and grounding verification. ConfArena evaluates presentations slide by slide in a simulated conference room and was shown to match human system rankings while detecting injected presentation problems including falsified numbers, degraded figures, dropped slides, and shuffled slide order.

A focus group of 22 industry and academic participants at the 31st European Conference on Pattern Languages of Programs, People, and Practices (EuroPLoP 2026) concluded that architectural decision-making, accountability, and the authoring of architectural guardrails remain fundamentally human tasks. The report introduces 'harness engineering' as an emergent discipline for building the system that governs AI-assisted system creation, comprising validation mechanisms, knowledge layers, and company-specific standards. As AI development agents increasingly support and partially automate software architecture tasks, this consensus offers practitioners a concrete division of labor: humans own critical decisions and accountability while AI handles more routine work. The concept of harness engineering gives organizations an actionable framing for governing AI-assisted development, and the notion of cognitive debt warns of long-term risks to human understanding of systems. Participants agreed that criticality — understood as the combination of uncertainty and cost of change — serves as the universal criterion for calibrating how much human oversight is needed. The report also highlights cognitive debt, the progressive erosion of human understanding when AI-assisted decisions are accepted without full intellectual engagement, and notes directions for future work.

## [Sola Security Brain Beats Coding Agents on Cloud Investigations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30345) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates the Sola Security Brain, a purpose-built security intelligence layer, against two leading coding agents, Claude Code and OpenAI Codex, on 28 cloud-security investigation tasks in the same live AWS environment. The Security Brain achieved 0.549 ± 0.012 coverage versus 0.340 ± 0.011 for Claude Code and 0.281 ± 0.006 for Codex, leading 24 of 28 tasks at 17.8x and 20.8x lower cost per task. The results suggest that general-purpose coding agents, despite their growing capabilities, are not sufficient for enterprise cloud-security investigations, where population-level questions require complete inventory coverage rather than partial answers. This has direct implications for security teams deciding whether to deploy coding agents directly or invest in specialized security context layers. All three systems were scored by a blinded, tier-weighted, grounding-gated relative recall over one joint claim pool, so scores share a denominator, and the ordering was identical in every grading draw. The paper also identifies a failure pattern called sample-and-generalise: agents enumerate a fraction of a large population, assert an unhedged universal negative, and disclose the sample size only in metadata—for example, Claude Code claimed no bucket policies exist after checking four bucket families in a sweep that sampled 40 of roughly 5,000 buckets, in an account where 65 buckets carry a wildcard-principal read grant.

On an A100, it reports geometric-mean speedups over same-mode padding of 2.74x eager and 3.39x compiled across four BERT scales, 1.97x eager across four FCN backbones, and 2.40-4.32x on a four-block Pairformer-style workload, with peak allocation dropping from 38.08 to 5.41 GiB on a high-variation batch. If the reported speedups hold up, this could influence how PyTorch handles ragged batching, particularly for transformer and pair-representation workloads where padding costs multiply across axes. The abstraction keeps packed values together with tensor-backed partitions and logical dimension order, so broadcasting creates ragged axes, feature transformations retain them, and reductions consume them, all while preserving autograd support. The paper is a preprint without peer review, and the code is only promised for release upon publication, so the results have not yet been independently reproduced.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Deep learning models such as BERT process batches of variable-length sequences, and the standard approach pads every sample to the longest one in the batch so that dense tensor operations can be used. This padding wastes computation and memory, and the waste compounds when multiple ragged axes exist, for example when an explicit pair state allocates BN_max^2 positions instead of the sum of N_i^2. Packing removes the waste but loses the logical axes and sample boundaries that a flat buffer no longer exposes, which is the gap DanLing NestedTensor aims to fill.

## [CLUE Framework Resolves Contextual Uncertainty in Underspecified Robot Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30428) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CLUE (Closed-Loop contextual Uncertainty rEsolution), a framework that uses an LLM-derived policy to hypothesize task-relevant concepts and plans, then grounds them into actions via an online language-embedded map. Deployed on a Boston Dynamics Spot across three real indoor and outdoor environments and 15 tasks, CLUE achieved a success rate within 7 percentage points of an oracle policy and outperformed an LLM-enabled planner without closed-loop feedback by a 4x margin. This work addresses a meaningful gap in robotics and embodied AI by enabling robots to operate in unfamiliar environments with underspecified natural language goals, where the robot must jointly infer task success criteria, relevant information, and where that information exists. It could benefit researchers in robotics, natural language processing, and embodied AI seeking more robust language-conditioned policies. Supporting experiments show that simply building and then querying a language-enriched map is insufficient for complex contextual planning, achieving roughly one third of CLUE's success rate while requiring over 10x more VLM tokens. The tasks required object disambiguation, functional inference, and occlusion reasoning, and additional information is available at the project website.

**Background**: Foundation models give robots the ability to interpret natural language and reason about environmental context, but most language-conditioned policies assume goals are well-specified and that task-relevant information is provided upfront via a prior map. Operating in unfamiliar environments with underspecified tasks entails high contextual uncertainty, so CLUE uses closed-loop environment interaction to sequentially evaluate hypotheses and refine plans as new information is gathered. A language-embedded map is a representation that stores visual-language model embeddings so that open-vocabulary scene understanding and on-demand querying by text prompts are possible.

## [CARGO: Context-Aware Retrieval-Gated Evaluation for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30471) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30471v1) introduces CARGO, a context-aware retrieval-gated evaluation framework for agentic AI that addresses a failure mode called reference-instance divergence (RID), where reference-based LLM-as-a-judge systems wrongly penalize correct answers applied to different entities. The paper also releases CARGO-Bench, a 246-item perturbation-based diagnostic suite with ground truth by construction, on which the standard reference-based judge penalizes 100% of correct entity-transplanted answers (discrimination index DI ~ 0), while CARGO eliminates these false penalties (0/50) and raises DI to 0.58 [0.48, 0.68]. Production agentic systems operate over dynamic entities such as support cases, assets, and accounts, so literal reference-based judging systematically mislabels correct behavior as hallucination, making evaluation signals unreliable for deployment decisions. CARGO reframes evaluation as selective prediction gated by retrieval confidence, offering a principled way to measure agent reliability in production and potentially changing how teams benchmark and monitor deployed agents. CARGO assigns each claim a three-way status (supported, contradicted, unverifiable) and penalizes only contradictions, grounding factual judgments in the live instance's observed context rather than the retrieved reference. A rubric-swap control attributes most of the effect to context-grounded dimension definitions, but the paper candidly reports a limitation: the leniency protecting entity values suppresses detection of procedural corruptions (20% recall), and a post-hoc fix plus an LLM-as-annotator study with written guidelines and adjudication show the same blind spot.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-judge is a widely used evaluation paradigm in which a large language model scores another model's outputs, often by comparing them against a reference answer; it is popular because it scales better than human review. Selective prediction is a classic machine learning idea in which a system may abstain from judging when confidence is too low, trading coverage for reliability. Agentic AI refers to LLM-driven systems that take actions and retrieve information across changing real-world entities, which makes static reference answers a poor fit for evaluation.

## [Evolutionary Persona Sets Boost LLM Output Diversity by 78.8%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30492) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30492) formulates persona diversification as a set-level conditioning problem and introduces four methods spanning coverage and dispersion subset selection, uniform-coverage sampling, and evolutionary persona generation. On the Alternative Uses Task, evolutionary persona generation increased response diversity by 78.8%, originality by 26.1%, flexibility by 49.5%, and holistic creativity by 13.9% over task-only prompting while maintaining 98.5% validity. Homogeneous LLM responses can cause groupthink and converge on suboptimal ideas, so this work offers a task-agnostic mechanism for eliciting divergent outputs that could benefit creative writing, brainstorming, and synthetic data generation. It also shows persona diversification composes with creativity-optimized prompting, adding 18.6% more response diversity and 6.3% more creativity, making it a reusable complement to prompt engineering. The design space is organized along two orthogonal axes: selecting versus generating personas, and space-filling versus frontier-seeking diversity. Evaluations span the Alternative Uses Task, Infinity-Chat, and the Divergent Association Task, with evolutionary personas nearly doubling persona-induced response separation on Infinity-Chat compared to random personas.

## [Softmax Attention Shown to Implement Policy Mirror Descent as a Closed-Loop Controller](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30500) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30500) proves that causal softmax attention can implement negative-entropy policy mirror descent (PMD) as a repeated controller, not just a one-step algebraic identity. The authors construct a fixed causal-softmax actor-environment-one-step-critic protocol and show that separately trained pre-LN Transformers empirically recover the target computation, with the learned actor reaching median returned-policy loss 1.052× the Exact PMD oracle in a preregistered five-run S=4 test. This work bridges reinforcement learning theory and Transformer interpretability by showing that the softmax attention mechanism naturally performs a well-known policy optimization algorithm, potentially explaining why Transformers are effective in sequential decision-making tasks. It could influence how researchers design and analyze attention-based controllers in RL and closed-loop control. The construction requires finite-logit/full-support domains, external tokenization and sampling boundaries, and mean-zero LayerNorm carrier conditions; at S=8, replacing the exact critic with a learned critic raises median T=20 loss to 0.0225 but still leaves Liang-Lai and Algorithm Distillation adaptations 20.2–24.2× higher-loss, though this is a one-sided sampled-critic bound because PolicyAttention consumes 144 generative transitions per round versus 20 on-policy transitions for the adaptations.

**Background**: Policy mirror descent (PMD) is a unifying framework in reinforcement learning that generalizes policy gradient methods via Bregman divergence and has linear convergence guarantees under regularization. This paper connects the two by showing that the statewise PMD update, softmax(log π + ηQ), can be compiled into a causal softmax attention computation.

## [Inquesto Score: A Reliability Protocol for Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30514) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Inquesto Score (IS), a protocol that measures voice-agent reliability as the percentage of calls in a fixed, versioned evaluation population that achieve the caller's goal without a functional failure or worse. Version 0.1 evaluates 30 scenarios, three acoustic conditions, four speaker groups, and 306 calls per agent across 13 configurations of a reference voice-agent system, with the protocol, reference implementation, and evaluation records released publicly. Voice agents are increasingly deployed in workflows where failed interactions can affect transactions and access, yet the field lacks reproducible and interpretable evaluation standards. A protocol with explicit failure definitions and severity levels could influence how the industry benchmarks and certifies voice AI before deployment. Rather than combining heterogeneous metrics into a single number, IS measures timing failures such as talk-over and delayed responses directly from audio, while semantic and state-dependent failures are judged using scenario predicates, tool traces, and a pinned open-model judge. Diagnostic views of behavior, acoustic robustness, identity handling, and speaker groups accompany the score without being merged into it, and the authors emphasize that reliable measurement requires evidence beyond transcripts and validation of the evaluators themselves.

## [First Convergence Guarantees for the Muon Optimizer, Plus a Nesterov Variant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30546) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30546) establishes the first asymptotic convergence guarantees for the Muon optimizer by modeling its Newton-Schulz iteration more accurately than the usual matrix sign function proxy. The authors prove that with suitable hyperparameters the iterates satisfy lim ||∇f(x_k)|| = 0, and under a global Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition the function values converge linearly; they also introduce Muesterov, a Nesterov-based variant of Muon with the same guarantees. Muon has recently become a popular optimizer for training neural networks, including large language models, but it previously lacked theoretical convergence guarantees, so this work provides a rigorous foundation for a widely used algorithm. The analysis could influence future optimizer design and give practitioners more confidence in Muon's stability and hyperparameter choices. The key insight is that the regularization implicit in Muon's Newton-Schulz implementation induces a bounded preconditioner, exposing Muon as a preconditioned Polyak heavy-ball method amenable to classical Lyapunov analysis. Numerical experiments on a scalar cross-entropy problem corroborate the theory and clarify how the learning rate and Newton-Schulz regularizer jointly control convergence, with preliminary nanoGPT training simulations offering practical intuition.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Muon is an optimizer for the 2D weight matrices of neural network hidden layers that uses a Newton-Schulz iteration to approximately orthogonalize the update, which acts as an implicit preconditioner and has been reported to speed up LLM pretraining. The Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition is a relaxation of strong convexity that bounds the suboptimality gap by the squared gradient norm, allowing linear convergence proofs without convexity. Heavy-ball and Nesterov methods are classical momentum-based gradient methods, and this paper recasts Muon within that framework.

## [Audit Finds Output-Based Monitors Match Latent Probes for AV Failure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30557) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30557) audits latent-space failure monitors across two autonomous-driving tasks: online vectorized map generation with LaneSegNet and end-to-end planning with VAD. It reports that a supervised latent probe reaches AUROC 0.780 for high Chamfer error on LaneSegNet and 0.868 for mean-ADE failure on VAD, but that output-only monitors achieve 0.825 and 0.924 respectively, with no statistically resolved improvement from adding latent features. The result challenges the assumption that access to a model's internal representations is necessary for strong runtime failure prediction, which matters for safety certification of autonomous-driving stacks where internal-state monitors are costly to build and validate. It suggests teams should first test cheap output-based baselines before investing in elaborate latent-space monitoring. The audit covers a broad suite of planning failure endpoints, including some whose labels depend on geometry unavailable to the non-latent baseline, yet the output-based monitors still match latent probes. The authors propose an evaluation protocol for testing the incremental value of latent access and release per-frame failure endpoint labels; the work is a focused empirical study rather than a new architecture.

The framework is motivated by the insight that memory is reconstructive and shaped by interference, source reliability, reinforcement, and reactivation, and it decomposes correctness into behavioral profiles rather than a single score. The code is available at https://github.com/jq-ding/MemProbe, though the work is a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

## [Action Forcing: Training Controllable World Models from Unlabeled Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30595) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30595) proposes 'Action Forcing,' a method that recovers grounded egomotion control signals directly from ordinary unlabeled video by tracking pixel displacements and applying principal components analysis (PCA), without any action annotations or trained encoders. The leading PCA components yield signed, scalable, composable throttle-yaw controls, and an online latent critic distills a frozen decoder-tracker-PCA teacher into a video DiT to prevent it from exploiting pixel-level supervision. Controllable world models typically require synchronized action annotations that are expensive or impossible to collect at scale, so a method that turns raw video into action-supervised training data could substantially lower the cost of building controllable generative simulators for robotics and embodied AI. The paper also critiques standard video-generation metrics and introduces reference-free measures of controllability, plausibility, conjuring, and geometric integrity, which may push the field toward more meaningful evaluation. The method can only recover motion axes actually represented in the training data, and despite backwards actions making up less than 1% of the data, the model learns to reverse, scale its response linearly, and compose throttle with steering. The online latent critic distills the frozen decoder-tracker-PCA teacher without backpropagating through the decoder or tracker, and the paper reports that most baselines follow familiar action directions but struggle to reverse or remain stationary.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: World models are learned generative models of an environment's dynamics that can be conditioned on actions to simulate future observations, and training them to be controllable normally requires paired video and action data collected from instrumented platforms or costly manual annotation. Egomotion refers to the motion of the camera itself through a scene, which induces a coherent pattern of pixel displacements across frames; PCA is a classical dimensionality-reduction technique that finds the dominant axes of variation in data. Video DiT refers to Diffusion Transformer architectures adapted for video generation, which are high-capacity models prone to exploiting low-level pixel supervision if not regularized.

## [Causal Retention in Interactive Agents: Theory and Causal Core System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30650) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30650) formalizes causal retention for interactive agents, proving that the optimal mechanism-probe error is a Bayes decision risk that vanishes exactly when every learning-interface fiber lies within one probe-answer fiber. The authors implement these conditions in a system called Causal Core and validate it on finite causal systems, continuous simulators, an official TD-MPC2 world model, and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, where a frozen last-layer probe reaches 0.958 balanced accuracy on source mechanisms but only 0.583 on changed delays, while the gated mechanism state reaches 1.000. The work argues that causal retention is distinct from task sufficiency and source-domain decodability, suggesting that agents can perform well on a task while failing to retain the causal mechanisms needed to adapt to changed conditions. This has implications for reinforcement learning, agent design, and causal inference, especially for building agents that remain reliable when delays, contexts, or intervention mechanisms shift. The paper provides a posterior-coverage theorem for budgeted retesting and an exact edit decomposition showing that the shifted set is the unique support of an error-free target update. In TD-MPC2, five target states per actuator recover effect-sign accuracy from 0.057 to 0.948 without degrading stable responses, and the gated mechanism state accepts only 0.056 of synchronized-readout candidates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Structural causal models (SCMs) are mathematical frameworks that use directed graphs and structural equations to represent causal mechanisms in a system. Bayes decision risk is the minimum expected loss achievable by an optimal decision rule under a prior distribution. In this context, causal retention asks whether a frozen learned state can answer a mechanism-probe map fixed independently of training, covering action, context, direct target, value, and delay.

## [Werracle: Sub-Cent On-Chain AI Oracle for EVM Smart Contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30719) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30719) introduces Werracle, a zero-storage on-chain AI decision oracle that fits inside a single 32-byte EVM storage slot (bytes32) and evaluates a 16-point Pareto micro-grid in only 21,438 gas, costing under $0.0005 on Layer-2 rollups like Base and Arbitrum. It derives non-linear decision hyperplanes from a 24-byte coordinate triplet using Mandelbrot escape dynamics and fixed-point Q16.16 arithmetic, and demonstrates a Uniswap v4 dynamic swap fee governor (WerracleFeeHook.sol) that adjusts LP fees between 0.05% and 0.50% based on real-time orderbook turbulence. This work addresses a critical gap in DeFi security: flash-loan attacks, sandwich MEV, and LVR occur atomically within a single block, and existing ZK-ML oracles with 10–300 second proving latency cannot react in time. By enabling sub-millisecond intra-block reflex actions at negligible cost, Werracle could allow smart contracts to defend against atomic exploits and dynamically manage liquidity, potentially reshaping how DeFi protocols handle real-time risk. The oracle uses pure Solidity bytecode with fixed-point Q16.16 arithmetic (WerrMath.sol) and is formally verified against a 1,000-test deterministic suite with a 100% pass rate, operating on a dedicated EVM devnet sandbox (Chain ID 4242). However, the abstract is truncated and lacks full implementation and evaluation details, and the approach relies on procedural Mandelbrot dynamics rather than conventional neural networks, which may limit the complexity of decisions it can express.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Storing static floating-point neural network weights directly in EVM storage costs millions of gas, making direct on-chain AI inference impractical. ZK-ML offloads matrix multiplications to off-chain provers but introduces 10–300 seconds of SNARK proving latency and 250,000–500,000 gas per proof verification, which is too slow for atomic DeFi exploits. Werracle sidesteps this by using a tiny 24-byte coordinate triplet and Mandelbrot escape dynamics to generate decision boundaries without storing any weights on-chain.

## [TrafficImag: First Benchmark for Counterfactual Roadside Traffic Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30722) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced TrafficImag, the first benchmark for counterfactual roadside traffic video generation, combining a large-scale dataset of 9,022 annotated images, 7,043 deduplicated video clips, and 31,145 actor-centered history-future samples with an executable evaluation protocol. The protocol represents each intervention as an actor-level program specifying the target actor, intended behavior, legal route, interaction order, and temporal constraints, and evaluates four validity dimensions: initial-state correctness, route and behavior validity, interaction consistency, and non-target preservation. This benchmark fills a gap in roadside traffic datasets, which previously supported perception, forecasting, and visual question answering but not counterfactual video generation, and it offers a unified evaluation interface across heterogeneous foundation models. It is a valuable resource for autonomous driving and video generation research, enabling diagnosis of behavior reasoning and conditional generation beyond perceptual video quality. Across state-of-the-art foundation models, the strongest reasoner reaches 80.4% macro F1, and the complete condition interface raises end-to-end success from 23.3% to 55.0% for the best generator. Oracle studies show that conditional video execution remains the primary bottleneck, and an end-to-end counterfactual is considered successful only when all four validity dimensions are satisfied.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Counterfactual video generation creates modified futures in which a selected actor's behavior is changed while the rest of the scene should remain consistent with road topology and unrelated traffic. Roadside traffic datasets have traditionally been used for perception, trajectory forecasting, and visual question answering, but they lack a way to evaluate whether generated counterfactual futures are physically and legally plausible. TrafficImag addresses this by pairing a large-scale roadside dataset with an executable protocol that unifies behavior reasoning, intervention-aware image editing, and conditional video generation.

## [New RVDSA Metric Optimizes Surgical Continuum Robot Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30745) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces the Reachable Volumetric Dexterous Solid Angle (RVDSA) metric, which measures a robot end effector's ability to reach points in a goal volume from multiple directions via collision-free paths, and combines it with an asymptotically optimal simulated annealing optimizer to design surgical continuum robots. Applied to a bimanual dexterous sheath robot for removing cancerous polyps in colon anatomies, the method achieved 78% higher RVDSA on average than optimizing for 3D voxel coverage alone. This work addresses a core challenge in surgical robotics: designing continuum robots that are dexterous enough for specific patient anatomies rather than generic workspaces. The anatomy-aware optimization could lead to more effective and safer robot-assisted procedures, particularly in confined spaces such as the colon, and the RVDSA metric may become a reusable benchmark for future medical robot design. The method relies on a computationally efficient motion planner to evaluate RVDSA for a given design and uses simulated annealing, an asymptotically optimal global optimizer, to search the design space. The reported 78% improvement is measured against a baseline that optimizes only 3D voxel coverage, and the validation is limited to simulated colon anatomies for a bimanual dexterous sheath robot.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Continuum robots are flexible manipulators with continuously curving structures and effectively infinite degrees of freedom, inspired by trunks, snakes, and tentacles, which lets them navigate confined spaces that rigid-link robots cannot reach. In surgery, this makes them attractive for procedures inside tubular organs such as the colon, but their many degrees of freedom make choosing geometric design parameters difficult. Dexterity metrics quantify how well a robot can orient and position its end effector, and solid angle is a measure of how much of the surrounding directional space is covered from a given point.

Researchers introduced SustainAI, a closed-loop framework that integrates real-time water metering, a hallucination-aware penalty model, and a water-aware routing algorithm to reduce the environmental footprint of AI inference. Evaluated on small language models extracting health misinformation, it revealed an 11-fold variation in water footprint across geographically distributed data centers, ranging from 0.0477 mL to 0.5360 mL per inference. This work addresses the often-overlooked water consumption of AI, which is rarely considered alongside energy and carbon impacts, and provides a practical foundation for integrating environmental accountability into AI infrastructure design. It could influence how AI deployments are routed and managed across regions facing different levels of water stress. Across 1,335 inference runs, the system consumed approximately 399 mL of water but produced only 240 correct outputs, highlighting substantial resource waste on inaccurate responses. The framework also extends beyond technical optimization through a Care by Design lens, framing AI sustainability around relational ethics, regional equity, and ecological stewardship.

**Background**: Data centers consume significant freshwater for cooling and electricity generation, yet this water footprint is largely invisible compared to energy and carbon metrics. SustainAI combines water monitoring, adaptive accountability, and ethical design principles to make AI deployment more environmentally responsible.

## [iDriveVLA Tackles Evaluation Bottleneck in Multi-Modal Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30818) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces iDriveVLA, a multi-modal planning framework that identifies a 'generation-evaluation asymmetry' in autonomous driving: planners generate strong candidate trajectories but fail to reliably select the best one. iDriveVLA combines a Safety-aware Scorer with a VLM-guided Modulator and an oracle-aligned progressive training strategy, achieving 94.95 PDMS on the NAVSIM v1 leaderboard, surpassing the human-expert reference. This work shifts attention from improving trajectory generation to improving trajectory evaluation, arguing that evaluation is the real bottleneck in multi-modal planning. If validated, this insight could redirect research in autonomous driving and robotics toward better scoring and selection mechanisms, potentially unlocking large unrealized planning potential. The unified trajectory evaluator consists of a Safety-aware Scorer for quality and risk estimation plus a VLM-guided Modulator for scene-adaptive criterion weighting. Training follows three stages: candidate imitation pretraining, candidate space refinement, and semantic ranking alignment; results are reported on the public NAVSIM v1 benchmark.

**Background**: Multi-modal planning represents multiple plausible future behaviors, which helps autonomous vehicles handle ambiguous and long-tail scenarios. NAVSIM is a public benchmark that evaluates planning performance using the PDMS metric, where higher scores indicate safer and more reliable driving.

A new arXiv preprint proposes SICMD (Subject-Invariant Cross-Modal Perceived Speech Decoding), a unified method that integrates fMRI and MEG signals to decode perceived speech. It reports improvements of over 10.6% in Top-1, 10.1% in Top-10, and 1.7% in Rank accuracy over baselines in cross-subject settings, while cutting training costs by 88.8% and 60.5% compared to multi-subject and intra-subject decoding. Cross-subject generalization is a major bottleneck for non-invasive brain-computer interfaces, since models trained on one person rarely transfer to another. By jointly addressing rich spatiotemporal representation and subject invariance, SICMD could make speech BCIs more practical and reduce the heavy per-subject calibration burden. The study uses a dataset of fMRI and MEG recordings from 12 subjects listening to the same continuous Chinese speech across 60 trials, with fMRI acquired at a 0.71-second repetition time. The authors systematically analyze fusion method, fusion position, encoder architecture, and model inputs, and visualization experiments support the approach's effectiveness.

Cross-modal learning here means using one modality to help decode another, and subject-invariant decoding means the model must work on people it was not trained on.

## [MOPD-Router Enables Token-Level Teacher Routing Without Domain Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30837) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MOPD-Router introduces a token-level routing framework for multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) that removes the need for prompt-level domain labels and uses the full teacher pool at every token. It also proposes ExpertAlign, a specialization-aware metric that scores each teacher by whether its correction to the student reflects the expertise acquired during post-training, outperforming Entropy and Novelty baselines across four distillation settings. This work shows that token-level routing can exploit cross-domain complementary supervision, reducing reliance on expensive prompt-level domain assignment and enabling the use of unlabeled training mixtures. It offers a practical improvement for integrating specialized teacher models into a single student, which matters for model compression and knowledge distillation researchers. On unlabeled data, ExpertAlign improves the overall score by 5.88 points (+12.3%) over Mean aggregation, and on domain-labeled data it outperforms standard MOPD by 3.95 points (+7.8%) without using the available domain labels. The framework requires no separate routing model and provides a plug-in interface supporting different metrics for selecting and weighting teacher-specific OPD signals.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) trains a single student model by having it generate rollouts and then receiving supervision from multiple specialized teacher models. Conventional practice hard-routes each prompt to a domain-matched teacher for the entire rollout, which requires prompt-level domain labels and ignores complementary signals from other teachers. On-policy distillation means the student is corrected on its own generated tokens rather than only on a fixed offline dataset.

## [EoupCT Estimates Unknown Pre-training Gradients to Curb LLM Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30935) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30935) proposes EoupCT, a framework that estimates unknown pre-training gradients by generating pseudo data via a learnable soft prompt with Gumbel-Softmax relaxation, then enforces orthogonality between new-task updates and those estimated gradients using a first-order Pareto optimizer. Experiments across multiple LLMs show it preserves both task-specific proficiency and general-purpose knowledge better than existing orthogonal gradient projection methods. Continual fine-tuning is how deployed LLMs adapt to new tasks, but it silently erodes general reasoning and safety behaviors; EoupCT targets the root cause by approximating the otherwise inaccessible pre-training signal. If it generalizes, it could make sequential fine-tuning of proprietary or open-weight models substantially safer without needing the original pre-training data. The method couples a learnable soft prompt with Gumbel-Softmax relaxation to synthesize pseudo data most susceptible to forgetting, then solves a multi-objective problem with a first-order efficient Pareto optimizer that jointly updates LLM parameters and the prompt. The abstract is truncated and does not report specific model sizes, benchmark names, or forgetting metrics, so quantitative comparisons remain unclear.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Catastrophic forgetting occurs because an LLM's weights are a single interconnected system: updating them for a new task can overwrite knowledge and behaviors learned earlier, including general reasoning and safety alignment. Orthogonal gradient projection is a common continual-learning remedy that constrains new-task updates to directions that do not interfere with old-task gradients, but it normally requires access to the old tasks' data or gradients. For off-the-shelf pre-trained LLMs, the original pre-training data and gradients are unknown and highly diverse, which is the gap EoupCT tries to fill.

## [Spackle Uses Adaptive Gaussians to Fix Large-View Single-Image NVS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30941) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Spackle, a lightweight residual learning framework for single-image novel view synthesis (NVS) that mitigates capacity competition in hybrid feedforward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) plus diffusion pipelines. Spackle works in three stages: predicting base 3DGS attributes, automatically identifying poorly reconstructed regions, and learning a residual 3DGS optimized only for those areas, then combining baseline and augmented Gaussians at inference. The authors report state-of-the-art performance on large-view-deviation cases. Large-view-deviation NVS from a single image is important for practical 3D content creation, AR/VR, and robotics, where only one photo may be available. By avoiding the fidelity loss caused by fixed Gaussian budgets, Spackle could make hybrid 3DGS-plus-diffusion systems more reliable for extreme viewpoint changes without sacrificing inference efficiency. The core problem is capacity competition: a fixed number of Gaussians forces resources to shift from visible to newly disoccluded areas, degrading original scene fidelity as the target view deviates further from the input. Spackle addresses this with a residual 3DGS branch trained exclusively on poorly reconstructed regions, which is then merged with the baseline at inference; the paper claims this remains lightweight and efficient.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Novel view synthesis (NVS) aims to generate photorealistic images of a scene from viewpoints not present in the input images. 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is a real-time radiance field rendering technique that represents scenes as many 3D Gaussians and has become popular since 2023. Hybrid decoupled frameworks combine feedforward 3DGS with diffusion models to handle large viewpoint changes, but they must allocate a limited number of Gaussians across both visible and newly disoccluded regions. Residual learning, familiar from ResNets, trains a network to predict a correction on top of a base output rather than the full output.

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.30946) introduces OneWorld, a shared-mechanism counterfactual generation framework that jointly models multiple action-conditioned futures under a common latent physical mechanism. A physical mechanism interpreter infers a distribution over latent mechanisms from each action-outcome branch, aggregates them into shared-world evidence, and uses this evidence to constrain flow training and guide sampling. World models that generate independently plausible but physically incompatible futures can produce contradictory predictions across interventions, undermining their reliability for planning and decision-making in robotics and embodied AI. OneWorld's approach to enforcing cross-intervention physical consistency addresses a key limitation in model-based planning, potentially improving how agents reason about the underlying world. The framework introduces a multi-intervention evaluation protocol in controlled environments following the interaction settings of ACWM-Phys, assessing whether generated futures can be jointly explained by the same physical parameters while also measuring single-rollout prediction quality. Experiments show OneWorld improves cross-intervention physical consistency while maintaining competitive single-rollout prediction quality.

**Background**: Action-conditioned video world models predict how a scene evolves under different actions, which is essential for planning and interaction in dynamic environments. However, futures generated independently from the same initial scene may each look plausible while implying incompatible physical properties such as friction or mass, leading to contradictory predictions. Counterfactual generation involves imagining hypothetical outcomes under different interventions, and latent physical inference aims to recover hidden properties like mass or friction from observed dynamics.

Researchers introduced MVVBench, a benchmark built from real-world multi-camera datasets that evaluates multi-view video reasoning in vision-language models. Each question is deliberately monocular-ambiguous, meaning it cannot be answered from any single view, and most cannot be answered from any single moment, so it is only solvable by jointly reasoning across views and time. Existing video benchmarks largely test single-view understanding, so MVVBench fills a gap by rigorously probing 4D (space plus time) reasoning that is essential for embodied perception and robotics. It also provides error analyses and inference-time strategies that could guide future multimodal model development. The benchmark probes six capabilities: implicit and explicit attribute identification, implicit and explicit relative distance, relative camera pose, and compositional counting, with human-authored and rigorously verified QA. The authors also show that task-specific chain-of-thought scaffolds and structured cross-view evidence aggregation yield substantial gains without retraining, and that reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards can elicit some latent multi-view competence.

Multi-view video understanding requires integrating spatial and temporal evidence across multiple, often non-overlapping camera streams, tracking entities as they move between viewpoints and reasoning about latent 4D continuity rather than any single visible frame.

## [Uniform Discrete Diffusion May Not Need Time Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30977) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30977) shows that explicit time conditioning in uniform discrete diffusion models (UDMs) is often unnecessary in practice. The authors prove that while the population-optimal UDM predictor generally depends on time, this dependence becomes negligible in finite-data language settings, and empirically time-agnostic predictors remain competitive with or outperform time-conditioned models across datasets and training objectives. This finding challenges a core design assumption in discrete diffusion language models, suggesting that removing time conditioning could simplify architectures and reduce computational overhead without sacrificing quality. If validated, it could influence future model design in the rapidly growing area of discrete diffusion for language and multimodal generation. The theoretical argument hinges on the observation that when a corrupted training sequence stays much closer to its original clean sequence than to competing training sequences, the empirical-optimal predictor is nearly insensitive to time over most of the diffusion trajectory, with the guarantee weakening only toward the high-noise endpoint. Trained language UDMs indeed show limited time sensitivity over most of the trajectory, though the paper is a preprint and its claims have not yet been peer-reviewed or widely replicated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion models generate data by gradually denoising a corrupted signal, and in continuous settings they typically rely on explicit time conditioning to tell the network how much noise is present at each step. Uniform discrete diffusion models (UDMs) adapt this idea to discrete data such as text, where the corruption process operates over a finite state space, and they have been explored as a faster, self-correcting alternative to autoregressive language models. Time conditioning has long been treated as a necessary architectural component, so questioning its necessity is a notable departure from conventional wisdom.
