## [Temporal Voting with Ranked Preferences Lacks EJR Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30555) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30555) studies proportional representation in temporal voting where one candidate is selected per round and voters hold ranked preferences that may change over time. It defines a hierarchy of axioms by combining cutoff interpretations with temporal versions of JR, PJR, EJR, and PSC, and proves that no version of extended justified representation (EJR) can be guaranteed under ranked preferences, unlike the approval-ballot case. This is a solid theoretical contribution to computational social choice, showing that moving from approval ballots to ranked preferences fundamentally weakens what proportionality guarantees are achievable in sequential decision-making. It matters for researchers designing temporal or sequential selection rules, such as participatory budgeting over time or repeated committee elections, because it delineates the exact limits of proportionality and the cost of flexibility in voter cutoffs. With a fixed common cutoff, JR, PJR, and PSC can be guaranteed, but only by rules that see all preferences in advance; once the cutoff may vary across rounds, even such rules cannot guarantee JR or PSC for groups that agree in only some rounds. Under individual cutoffs, no version of JR or PJR can be guaranteed, yet serial dictatorship achieves PJR up to an additive loss that no rule can improve on, and checking the axioms is often coNP-complete while a stronger axiom can be easier to check.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Temporal voting is a setting where a sequence of single-winner decisions is made over time, rather than selecting one committee all at once. Proportional representation axioms such as justified representation (JR), proportional JR (PJR), extended JR (EJR), and proportionality for solid coalitions (PSC) formalize how well groups of like-minded voters are represented; PSC is a classical criterion for ranked voting systems introduced by Dummett in 1984. Prior work on temporal voting focused on approval ballots, where voters simply approve or disapprove of candidates, but this paper considers ranked preferences, which are more expressive and may change between rounds.

## [MedTokenBudget Routes Lesion-Enriched Tokens for Dermoscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30613) ⭐️ 6.0/10

MedTokenBudget introduces a supervised post-backbone token routing framework that learns to construct compact lesion-enriched representations for dermoscopic image classification when auxiliary lesion masks are available. Its Lesion-Aware Token Scoring (LATS) module fuses attention entropy, feature norm, and local feature contrast through a learned scorer, then routes the top-K patches under a target budget, trained with budget curriculum learning, diversity regularization, attention distillation, and lesion-mask supervision. Existing token pruning methods reduce tokens using generic saliency or similarity signals but rarely verify whether the retained subset still contains the lesion, so this work directly targets diagnostic evidence preservation. It could improve the efficiency of Vision Transformer-based dermoscopy classifiers while reducing the risk of discarding clinically relevant regions. The trained router is evaluated with a lesion retention rate that directly measures how much ground-truth lesion evidence survives the token budget, and on ISIC 2019 mask-supervised LATS consistently outperforms Random and ToMe at headline budgets while retaining substantially more lesion patches. Code is provided for reproducibility, with complete tabulated results in the supplementary material.

**Background**: Vision Transformers split an image into patches and process all of them uniformly, which is computationally expensive even though diagnostic evidence in dermoscopy is concentrated in the lesion region. Token pruning methods selectively remove uninformative tokens to cut memory and latency, but generic importance criteria may discard lesion patches. Dermoscopy is a non-invasive skin imaging technique used for skin cancer diagnosis, and ISIC 2019 is a widely used public benchmark of dermoscopic images.

## [Paper Warns of 'Authorship Hazard' When LLM Agents Write Their Own Governance Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30614) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30614) identifies an 'authorship hazard' in agentic dataspaces: LLM agents that are both subjects of governance policies and authors of those policies can write the norms that bind them. The authors propose a single principle—an agent is a subject of the governance plane, never an author of it—and show that on a frozen corpus of agent drafts, publishing without approval reverses 80 authorization decisions, most via drafts that only change a field's sensitivity classification. This matters because dataspaces are increasingly proposed as the coordination layer for agentic AI, and if agents can author the policies that govern them, the entire enforcement model becomes circular and untrustworthy. The paper gives AI governance researchers and practitioners a concrete design principle and shows why policy-diff classifiers alone cannot catch subtle authorship attacks. The paper reports that a classifier reading the policy diff misses every such draft necessarily, while treating classification as authorship routes them all to review; the required registry-held classification is designed and modelled but not yet implemented in the prototype. At the execution boundary, protected fields reach the model in 105 of 105 cases under prompt-stated duties and in 0 of 105 when the ODRL duty is compiled into an invocation-time tool-call constraint, though where the value is not confined to a named field the compiled condition exposes it in 7 of 7 cases.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Dataspaces are federated data-sharing architectures in which connectors decide whether a data transfer may occur, typically enforcing machine-readable policies such as ODRL. As LLM agents gain the ability to compose tool calls and spawn sub-agents, they can also generate governance artifacts like policies and classifications, creating a gap between research on artifact quality and research on who may authorize an artifact for use. The paper argues that publication itself is a governance event, so an agent that both drafts and is bound by a policy effectively writes the norms that constrain it.

A new arXiv preprint (2609.30690) proposes a three-stage framework for multi-UAV aerial base station networks that combines a threat-aware K-means (TAKM) algorithm, an optimal matching stage, and a threat-aware multi-agent twin delayed deep deterministic policy gradient (MATD3) algorithm to jointly optimize trajectories, power, and user associations. Simulations report zero observed safety violations while achieving superior global energy efficiency and faster convergence than other learning methods and non-clustering baselines. Energy efficiency and operational safety are two of the biggest obstacles to deploying UAVs as flying base stations, especially in threat-prone environments. This work shows that combining clustering-based initialization with multi-agent reinforcement learning can deliver zero safety violations and generalization to unseen user distributions and larger fleets, which could make aerial base station deployments more practical for coverage and capacity enhancement. The framework first uses TAKM to determine the minimum number of UAVs and safe initial placements, then applies optimal matching to assign physical UAVs to centroids while minimizing energy expenditure, and finally uses threat-aware MATD3 for dynamic trajectory, power, and user-association optimization. It outperforms greedy particle swarm optimization (GPSO) and matches optimized PSO (OPSO) while incurring significantly lower online deployment computational complexity, though results are limited to simulation scenarios.

## [VLALight: A 0.5B Vision-Language-Action Model for Traffic Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30709) ⭐️ 6.0/10

VLALight is a lightweight end-to-end vision-language-action framework that maps multi-view intersection observations and signal-phase information directly to discrete traffic signal actions using a compact 0.5B-parameter model, without intermediate image-to-text descriptions or handcrafted traffic-state representations. Experiments show it reduces pooled emergency-vehicle waiting time by 21.1% over the cascaded VLMLight baseline while running in real time on local hardware and generalizing to unseen intersection topologies and traffic-flow patterns. Existing VLM-based traffic signal control systems are loosely coupled and repeatedly convert information between modules, which loses fine-grained visual details and introduces substantial inference latency. VLALight's direct end-to-end action prediction with a small model could make visual-context-aware signal control practical for real-time deployment on local intersection hardware, benefiting intelligent transportation and smart-city applications. The model fuses multiple directional camera views into a unified visual input and uses textual instructions to establish correspondence between views, traffic movements, and signal phases, enabling direct action prediction without intermediate representations. It is an arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.30709v1) that has not yet undergone peer review, and its reported gains are measured against the cascaded VLMLight baseline rather than against a broad set of production controllers.

## [Gemma 4 Study: Source Framing Beats Document Order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30716) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30716) presents a behavioral evaluation of Google's pre-trained Gemma 4-e4b model using a counterbalanced design with 13 items and 784 forward passes. It finds that when two conflicting documents are given, source framing (e.g., 'official guideline' vs. 'fresh update') significantly outweighs the document's presentation order in determining the model's answer. This work highlights that LLM judgments are highly sensitive to how sources are semantically framed, which has direct implications for retrieval-augmented generation, fact-checking, and any pipeline where models must reconcile conflicting documents. It also suggests that simple prompt-ordering tricks are less reliable than controlling source framing when trying to steer model prioritization. The study used a completely counterbalanced design to mathematically isolate framing and position effects while canceling vocabulary biases, and found a primacy effect that varied by at least a factor of 5 depending on surface wording. The primacy bias was driven mainly by overall structural repetition (verbatim templates) rather than short copy-cues like 'is [Answer]', and the small sample (n=13) and single-model focus limit generalizability.

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used to evaluate or summarize text, raising questions about whether their judgments are consistent and robust to framing effects. Source framing refers to how a document is described (e.g., official guideline vs. fresh update), while primacy effect refers to the tendency to favor the first item presented. Counterbalanced experimental design is a method that systematically varies the order and conditions of stimuli to cancel out order and vocabulary biases, allowing cleaner isolation of each factor's effect.

A new arXiv paper (2609.30783) proposes LIRSeg, which fully replaces explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT) with a compact set of learnable latent tokens for reasoning segmentation in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Trained in two stages via spatial alignment and GRPO with segmentation rewards, LIRSeg achieves absolute gIoU improvements of 4.9% on ReasonSeg, 7.1% on MUSE, and 4.7% on MMR over the VisionReasoner baseline, while reducing reasoning tokens by roughly 16x. This work targets a key inefficiency in multimodal reasoning: explicit verbal CoT introduces attention interference and inflates token cost during perception-token generation. By showing that compact latent tokens can outperform verbose reasoning on segmentation benchmarks, it strengthens the case for latent-space reasoning as a practical alternative to Chain-of-Thought in MLLMs, which could benefit interactive and embodied AI applications. To keep the compact latent tokens informative, LIRSeg introduces three mechanisms from an information perspective: extreme-advantage sampling for selecting informative training signals, decoupled exploration-stability updates for learning complementary representations, and latent diversity amplification to prevent representational collapse. The paper reports code in supplementary materials, but it is a niche arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

Existing MLLM approaches typically generate explicit Chain-of-Thought text before localizing the target, but those redundant textual tokens disrupt attention and increase the effective distance between visual tokens. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm popularized by DeepSeek for enhancing reasoning in LLMs, and latent reasoning (as in Meta FAIR's Coconut) replaces verbal intermediate steps with continuous hidden representations.

## [XPhysICS: Cross-Physical-Domain Threat Grounding for ICS Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30805) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.30805) introduces XPhysICS, a provenance-aware, target-conditioned method that determines whether cyber-physical threats documented for one industrial control system (ICS) plant can be structurally grounded and validated in another. It evaluates candidate mappings using five eligibility criteria — role compatibility, implemented type compatibility, stage coherence, slice viability, and rule-surface applicability — and tests them across 83 structured source-threat abstractions spanning water treatment, water distribution, hydro/water-energy, and chemical-process targets. ICS threats are increasingly reused across plants, but semantic similarity alone does not prove that a threat's physical effects are admissible or evaluable on a different target, so this work offers a more rigorous, traceable basis for cross-domain threat reuse. If validated, such grounding criteria could improve how defenders transfer threat intelligence between critical infrastructure sites without over-claiming applicability. The paper separates grounding acceptance, slice adequacy, dynamic realizability, consumer applicability, and consumer outcome as distinct evidence layers, and reports controlled target-side studies of SWaT-to-water-treatment and WADI-to-water-distribution groundings that yield clean, nominal-confounded, and near-threshold consumer outcomes. Nine Hydro/GRFICS cases extend bounded validation-slice execution, and the authors also evaluate predictive, state-aware, and phase-aware consumer lanes alongside the unmodified upstream GeCo implementation and a paper-derived reproduction of a physics-guided search method over three frozen groundings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Industrial control systems (ICS) are the hardware and software networks that monitor and regulate physical processes in critical infrastructure such as water, energy, and chemical plants, and their security is a specialized branch of cybersecurity often discussed alongside OT security. Threat modeling for ICS must account for cyber-physical effects, meaning a cyberattack's consequences on physical equipment and processes, not just on data. Provenance-aware methods track where threat knowledge came from and how it was derived, which helps avoid treating superficially similar threats as interchangeable. XPhysICS builds on this idea by requiring explicit, target-conditioned criteria before a threat from one plant is reused against another.

A new arXiv paper (2609.30863) presents a case study of a large embedded systems company transitioning toward becoming an AI-first organization, based on a mixed-method analysis of a semi-structured workshop with 40 participants including scrum masters, architects, management, and product owners. The findings show participants expect agentic AI to reshape team structure, required competencies, organizational strategy, and developer roles, and the paper offers a concrete roadmap plus implications for federated AI team formation and human-in-the-loop practices. This case study provides early empirical insight into how such a transition is perceived by practitioners, which can inform workforce planning and adoption strategies across the software industry. The study is qualitative and based on a single workshop with 40 participants, so it does not introduce new technical methods or quantitative benchmarks; its contribution is a roadmap and organizational implications rather than a validated framework. The paper specifically discusses federated AI team formation, human-in-the-loop practices, and sustainable adoption of AI agents in embedded software engineering.

## [Persistent Negatives Stabilize Black-Box On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30864) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30864) introduces persistent-negative adversarial distillation, a live-pool method that replaces a fraction of each discriminator batch with historical, prompt-matched teacher-student comparisons. This addresses the moving-target problem in black-box on-policy distillation, where discriminator negatives sampled from the latest student change after every policy update. The work identifies the discriminator's negative distribution as an important design axis in black-box on-policy distillation, potentially improving how student models learn from teacher responses when only sampled outputs, not token probabilities, are available. It could benefit researchers and practitioners working on LLM distillation and alignment with limited teacher access. Under matched discriminator compute, historical comparisons train the discriminator while GRPO remains on-policy with fresh student responses. The analysis identifies the Bayes-optimal reward as a teacher-to-negative log-density ratio and shows persistent negatives reduce reward-estimation MSE relative to fresh-negative training, with smoother discriminator trajectories and fewer below-chance dips across two student families, three judges, and four judged-chat benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Black-box On-Policy Distillation (OPD) aims to improve a student model using its own generations when the teacher only provides sampled responses, not token probabilities. Adversarial distillation learns a discriminator over prompt-matched teacher and student responses and uses its score as the policy reward, but sampling negatives from the latest student couples the reward to a distribution that shifts after every update. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a policy-gradient method used here to keep the student on-policy with fresh responses.

<a id="item-298"></a>
## [SemISAC Unifies Semantic Communication and Sensing in One ISAC Waveform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30891) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.30891) proposes SemISAC, a dual-function waveform that performs both semantic communication (SemCom) and semantic sensing within a single integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) signal. It uses a joint deep learning semantic encoder that extracts task-specific information for both functions, and is evaluated in a vehicular scenario where vehicles share pixel-wise road segmentation while simultaneously classifying surrounding objects and estimating their ranges. This work is significant because it moves beyond existing ISAC designs that apply semantic technology to only the communication or the sensing module, showing that a single waveform can serve both goals with minimal loss in communication quality. If validated, such unified designs could simplify 6G transceivers and enable cooperative vehicular perception, a key use case for future wireless networks. On the transmitter side, a deep learning encoder converts the input road-scene image into semantic symbols placed on the data cells of an OFDM grid, while the remaining cells serve as pilots for channel state information estimation and sensing; the pilot configuration is adaptively optimized based on channel conditions to balance communication and sensing requirements. At the receiver, a deep learning model reconstructs the segmentation, while the transmitting vehicle captures reflected waveforms from surrounding objects and uses task-specific deep learning decoders for target recognition and range estimation; simulations show segmentation accuracy close to a dedicated SemCom module while outperforming conventional and semantic baselines in target recognition and range estimation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) is a 6G concept that lets the same wireless waveform and protocol both transmit data and sense the environment, unlike 5G where sensing is a secondary feature. Semantic communication (SemCom) goes further by transmitting only task-relevant meaning rather than raw bits, often using deep learning encoders and decoders. OFDM is the widely used multicarrier waveform in Wi-Fi and 4G/5G, in which some subcarriers carry known pilot symbols so the receiver can estimate the wireless channel (channel state information). SemISAC combines these ideas by sharing one OFDM waveform between semantic data and sensing pilots.

## [Unified Protocol Reveals Robust Counterfactual Explanations Are Not Comparable Across Model Changes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30918) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.30918v2) proposes a unified cross-family evaluation protocol that holds factual instances and generated counterfactuals fixed while testing six robust counterfactual explanation methods and two standard baselines against the same eight types of model change on four tabular datasets. The study finds that relative performance and failure modes vary across change families, with bounded parameter perturbations altering only 0.95% of test predictions on average versus 4.9% for bootstrap retraining, and that methods with guarantees for one type of change do not necessarily transfer to others. This work exposes a methodological flaw in how robust counterfactual explanations are currently evaluated: reported robustness scores answer different questions depending on which model change a method was designed for, making cross-paper comparisons misleading. It matters for the XAI community because it argues for a common protocol that specifies model changes, measures their realized behavioral magnitude, and separates generation performance from robustness, which could reshape benchmarking standards for algorithmic recourse. The benchmark compares six robust methods and two standard baselines on four tabular datasets, characterizing every changed classifier through its outputs and reporting empirical robustness alongside coverage, base validity, and proximity. RobX transfers most consistently across change families in the experiments, though greater stability can require larger interventions, and the authors emphasize that generation performance should be kept separate from robustness.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Counterfactual explanations (CFEs) are a form of explainable AI that propose minimal changes to an input—such as altering income or credit history—that would cause a machine learning model to produce a different, often more favorable, prediction, thereby offering recourse to affected individuals. Because deployed models are frequently retrained or updated, researchers have developed robust CFE methods intended to keep working after the model changes, but these methods are typically evaluated only against the specific type of change they were designed for, such as small parameter perturbations or retraining on new data.

## [PORL Combines Online Pretraining and Offline RL for Job Shop Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30948) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new paper introduces PORL (Pretrained Offline Reinforcement Learning), a hybrid method that first learns a general scheduling policy through online interaction in simulation and then fine-tunes it offline on production-specific data using a KL-divergence-based policy constraint. Evaluated on Job Shop Scheduling Problem instances with distribution shift and datasets generated from heuristic, noisy-expert, and random behavioral policies, PORL consistently achieves lower optimality gaps than standalone offline RL and general scheduling baselines. The approach addresses a key industrial pain point: online exploration in real production scheduling is often impractical or costly, while pure offline RL is highly sensitive to dataset quality and coverage. By showing that offline adaptation of a pretrained policy reduces sensitivity to data quality, PORL suggests a practical path toward deploying RL in industrial scheduling environments. The method introduces a KL-divergence-based policy constraint to limit deviations from the pretrained policy during offline fine-tuning, and its advantage over standalone offline RL grows as dataset quality decreases. Evaluation covers JSSP instances with distribution shift and datasets from heuristic, noisy-expert, and random behavioral policies, though the paper does not report comparisons against state-of-the-art online RL or exact solvers on large-scale instances.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: The Job Shop Scheduling Problem (JSSP) is a classic combinatorial optimization problem in which n jobs, each consisting of ordered operations, must be processed on m machines to minimize makespan, and it is central to industrial production planning. Reinforcement learning can learn scheduling policies, but online RL requires repeated interaction with an environment, which is expensive or impossible in real factories, while offline RL learns only from a fixed historical dataset and is sensitive to its quality and coverage. PORL bridges these paradigms by pretraining online in simulation and then adapting offline to the target production distribution.

## [FLIP: Final-Layer Probing Method for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30993) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers introduced FLIP, a final-layer inference-time probe that applies elementwise flooring to the normalized hidden state of a vision-language model before logit computation, leaving parameters, prompts, and decoding unchanged. Sweeping intervention strength on a controlled detection/counting task revealed three regimes: negligible change, a bounded interior regime where detection recall at IoU 0.50 improves while tolerant counting error falls, and over-suppression. This work offers a formalized four-criterion probe-and-sweep protocol to discipline the interpretation of intervention effects, addressing the ambiguity of whether behavioral changes reflect improved use of visual evidence, generic instability, or degradation. It positions FLIP as a validation step for intervention-based mechanistic interpretability rather than a steering method, which could influence how researchers evaluate internal interventions in multimodal models. The protocol requires regime structure, grounding-proxy alignment, feature-coherence dependence, and failure to reproduce the same positive regime on a performance-based negative control. Raw decoder-layer interventions, including the last-block output before final normalization, and the singleton-pair left/right control fail to reproduce the Final-site signature, while same-site operators and multiple VLMs replicate it.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are multimodal systems that process both images and text, and their internal representations can be probed to understand how they use visual evidence. Inference-time intervention methods modify activations during inference without changing model weights, but interpreting the resulting behavioral changes is mechanistically ambiguous. FLIP focuses on the final normalized hidden state passed to the output head, which is the logit-facing site where the model converts representations into predictions.

## [G²PTQ refreshes gradient and Hessian estimates for better LLM quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31009) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers introduced G²PTQ, a unified post-training quantization (PTQ) framework that combines first- and second-order gradient compensation under a globally supervised, block-wise objective. Unlike prior global methods that freeze Hessian estimates at the start, G²PTQ refreshes gradient and Hessian estimates before quantizing each Transformer block and adds a trust-region scaling mechanism to prevent exploding weight updates. Post-training quantization is a practical way to cut the memory and compute footprint of large language models without retraining, and GPTQ-based methods are the de facto standard. G²PTQ's improvements could enable more accurate low-bit deployment of LLMs, benefiting practitioners who run models on constrained hardware. The framework derives efficient implementations for block-wise Hessian approximation and exact first-order gradient compensation, and its trust-region scaling dynamically bounds the gradient step. Experiments across various model families and bit-widths show better alignment with full-precision models and outperformance of state-of-the-art baselines; code is available at https://github.com/G2PTQ/G2PTQ.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training quantization (PTQ) compresses a trained LLM's weights to low precision, typically 4-bit integers, to reduce memory and computation without retraining. GPTQ is a widely used PTQ method that uses a second-order approximation of the loss via a Hessian estimate to minimize quantization error. Prior GPTQ-style methods either use local, layer-wise objectives without global supervision or global objectives that fix Hessian estimates at the start and ignore first-order gradients, causing guidance to grow stale as quantization proceeds.

## [Pocket-STVG: Lightweight Cascade Architecture for Spatio-Temporal Video Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31135) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Pocket-STVG (P-STVG) is a lightweight cascade architecture for spatio-temporal video grounding that combines efficient pre-trained components instead of large end-to-end models, using a MobileViCLIP-based temporal-aware video encoder, an MDETR-derived spatial encoder-decoder, and a shared aligned text encoder. It performs temporal localization via either a lightweight 1D U-Net or a simple thresholding strategy, enabling both weakly supervised and zero-shot operation, and achieves results on par with weakly supervised methods while improving on earlier zero-shot approaches with fewer than 90M parameters. This work shows that STVG can be tackled with a compact, indexing-friendly pipeline rather than expensive multimodal large language models, which could make video grounding far more practical for large-scale video collections and resource-constrained deployment. It also demonstrates a favorable performance-efficiency trade-off that may encourage more research into modular, pre-trained-component-based designs for multimodal video tasks. The architecture precomputes video representations independently of the query, yielding an indexing-friendly pipeline for efficient inference and large-scale video collections, and it uses fewer than 90M parameters. The same framework supports both weakly supervised and zero-shot settings through either a lightweight 1D U-Net or a simple thresholding strategy for temporal localization.

## [FedHisto-PAST v2: Stain-Aware Federated Learning for Lung Histopathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31150) ⭐️ 6.0/10

FedHisto-PAST v2 introduces a parameter-efficient, stain-aware federated learning framework that combines a frozen HIBOU-B pathology foundation model with stain-conditioned paired-view prediction, reliability-aware prototype learning, and adaptive federated aggregation. Evaluated on a five-client non-IID simulation and the exploratory LungHist700 cohort for three-way lung cancer classification, it achieved a Macro-F1 of 0.728560 and balanced accuracy of 0.730454 while updating only 1.253841% of model parameters. This work addresses two persistent obstacles in clinical AI: stain variation across pathology labs and non-IID data in federated settings, while keeping adaptation costs low by freezing a large foundation model. If validated further, such parameter-efficient federated pipelines could let hospitals collaboratively train diagnostic models without sharing raw patient slides. Internal performance was near-ceiling across all principal methods, limiting discrimination on the fixed split; on the external LungHist700 cohort, higher Normal and SCC recall came at the cost of lower ACA recall, and calibration remained imperfect. Ablation analysis found prediction-level consistency was the only component with a clearly supported independent contribution, while feature consistency and prototype regularization showed no conclusive gains.

Parameter-efficient fine-tuning adapts large foundation models by updating only a small fraction of parameters, reducing computation and communication costs.

## [Teacher-Anchored Selection of Quantized Models Under Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31155) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31155) studies how to choose among post-training quantized model candidates when target labels are absent or scarce under domain shift. It finds that minimum teacher distortion acts almost as a constant rule, always selecting the same eight-bit, per-channel, unclipped configuration, and that confidence-based estimators can order the candidate family nearly backwards in an overconfident-collapse regime. Deploying compressed models under distribution shift is common in practice, yet label-free selection among quantization candidates remains unreliable. The paper's finding that teacher-anchored selection reduces mean regret at small label budgets across 134 candidate families offers a practical, low-label strategy for model deployment. The advantage of anchoring fades beyond twenty-five labels, and the paper gives exact quadratic identities for a canonical quadratic analogue of the family. It also shows that under symmetric corruption, the label-dependent part of a criterion linear in the label indicator is multiplied by one common factor when its coefficient sums are candidate-invariant, a class that includes teacher contrasts and accuracy but not cross-entropy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training quantization compresses a trained model into several deployment candidates with different bit widths and granularity, and domain shift means the target data distribution differs from the source data used in training. Label-free model selection tries to pick the best candidate without target labels, often by measuring how much a candidate deviates from the original teacher model. Overconfident collapse refers to a regime where a network's predictions become extremely confident and poorly ordered, which can break confidence-based estimators.

## [SPADE: A New Metric for Measuring Serendipity in Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31164) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers introduced SPADE (Serendipitous Pareto Distance Evaluation), an offline metric that maps items into a two-dimensional popularity-similarity space and computes a user-specific Pareto frontier of maximally popular and historically similar items. The serendipity score is the average minimum Euclidean distance from this frontier, calculated only for correctly recommended test-set items, and was validated across five datasets and five baseline algorithms. Existing beyond-accuracy metrics often isolate either historical similarity or global popularity, so SPADE's combined popularity-similarity frontier offers a more robust way to evaluate serendipity. This could help recommender systems avoid gaming metrics with irrelevant or non-personalized recommendations, improving user exploration and satisfaction. SPADE computes the minimum Euclidean distance from a user-specific Pareto frontier strictly for correctly recommended test-set items, and experiments show it prevents algorithms from exploiting beyond-accuracy measures with irrelevant or non-personalized recommendations. The metric was tested on five datasets and five baseline algorithms, though the abstract does not specify which datasets or algorithms were used.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Recommender systems traditionally optimize accuracy metrics like precision or recall, but beyond-accuracy goals such as diversity, novelty, and serendipity are increasingly important for user satisfaction. Serendipity refers to surprising yet relevant recommendations that break predictable consumption cycles, but measuring it offline is challenging because it must balance similarity, popularity, and actual user relevance. SPADE addresses this by using a Pareto frontier concept from multi-objective optimization to define a boundary of items that are both popular and similar, so that recommendations far from this boundary are considered more serendipitous.

## [Metric-based Loss Weighting Boosts Visual Sensitivity in Multimodal LLM Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31169) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31169) introduces Metric-based Loss Weighting, a training method that increases the loss for tokens that benefit from accompanying images, identified via Point-wise Cross-mutual Information (PCXMI) and a new Congruency-based PCXMI metric. Fine-tuning three pretrained Multimodal Large Language Models on image-guided machine translation for three language directions improved accuracy on the CoMMuTE contrastive dataset by up to more than 7 percentage points over standard fine-tuning. Multimodal machine translation models often ignore the image signal even when it is available, so this work offers a practical training-time fix that improves visual grounding without changing model architecture. The gains on the contrastive CoMMuTE benchmark suggest the approach could help make multimodal LLMs more reliably use visual context in translation and other cross-modal tasks. ## [MoSAR Learns Adaptive Attention Geometry for Long-Context LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31261) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Mixture of Semantic Attention Regimes (MoSAR), which uses input-conditioned query and key routers after positional encoding to select mixtures over short, medium, and global attention regimes, inducing a continuous distance-dependent attention field instead of a fixed sparsity pattern. In controlled pre-training with matched 500M-parameter models, MoSAR improved perplexity over dense RoPE at the training context length and achieved the best perplexity under length extrapolation, including against ALiBi, while remaining stable under deterministic top-1 discretization. Long-context language modeling is bottlenecked by the quadratic cost of dense self-attention, and most efficient alternatives hard-code sparsity or locality in advance. MoSAR's data-learned geometry suggests attention approximation can be both adaptive and cheaply approximable at inference, which could influence how future efficient transformers are designed. MoSAR applies its routers after positional encoding and learns a controlled-decay geometry over query-key interactions, which can later be discretized via top-1 routing for low-cost inference. The experiments use matched 500M-parameter models and report gains at the training context length and under length extrapolation, though the abstract does not specify the exact context lengths or benchmark datasets used.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Standard self-attention compares every token with every other token, giving O(L²) complexity in sequence length L, which becomes prohibitive for long contexts. Efficient transformers typically avoid this by predefining sparse or local attention patterns, such as sliding windows or fixed block sparsity. Mixture-of-experts architectures instead use a gating network to route each input to specialized subnetworks, and MoSAR adapts this routing idea to select among attention regimes rather than experts.

UniAR improves average accuracy by 1.5 percentage points on MRI benchmarks and 1.2 percentage points on facial benchmarks over baselines, evaluated across four benchmarks covering brain MRI and facial expression scenarios. The framework combines multi-granularity prompt learning with a Mixture-of-Experts alignment module that dynamically routes vector-quantized visual prototypes to semantic representations at matching granularities.

A new concept paper (arXiv:2609.31313) proposes VLA-Dreamer, an architecture that trains a predictive world model directly on the embedding space of a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model's vision encoder rather than on pixel space. The authors hypothesize that these embeddings are action-relevant and can be used to predict future states, and they outline how the trained world model could support short-term planning by sampling VLA actions from goal images. VLA models currently require massive amounts of high-quality imitation learning data and lack an explicit world model, which limits both sample efficiency and confidence in their control capabilities. If embedding-space world models prove viable, they could substantially reduce data requirements and add planning ability to robot policies, affecting how robot learning systems are trained and deployed. The proposed loss is computed in the VLA's embedding space rather than pixel space, similar to Joint Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA), which avoids the cost of pixel-level reconstruction. The paper is explicitly a concept proposal with no empirical results yet; the authors frame the inability of VLA embeddings to predict future states as a key limitation they intend to test.

## [ECO Postprocessing Boosts Closed-Loop Driving Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31383) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Endpoint-Constrained Optimization (ECO), a lightweight postprocessing layer that anchors predicted trajectories to the vehicle's executed history while preserving the policy's predicted endpoint and reshaping intermediate waypoints for better feasibility. On HUGSIM, ECO improved VaVAM from 18.1 to 31.0 HD-Score (+71%), taking 1st place on the HUGSIM Closed-Loop Driving Challenge, and on AlpaSim it raised scene scores of VaVAM and DiffusionDrive by 123% and 22% respectively. This work identifies a complementary cause of the open-loop/closed-loop gap beyond covariate shift and causal confusion: waypoint-based supervision does not guarantee physically coherent intermediate trajectories. Because ECO requires no map, privileged simulator state, or additional training and can be inserted between many waypoint-emitting policies and their controllers, it offers a broadly applicable, low-cost way to improve closed-loop driving performance. ECO is a postprocessing layer that requires no map, privileged simulator state, or additional training, and it improves the aggregate closed-loop score of all six evaluated generative and regression-based driving policies across two closed-loop simulators, with gains tending to increase as base plans violate motion limits more often. The abstract notes that inconsistencies concentrate primarily at intermediate waypoints while the predicted endpoint remains comparatively reliable, which motivates preserving the endpoint.

## [Active Provenance Gate Blocks Fabricated Consensus in Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31422) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.31422) by Jakub Masłowski and a co-author introduces the Active Provenance Gate (APG), a post-debate verification layer that audits each claim in a multi-agent debate summary against the debate logs, applies self-correction, and blocks unsupported claims before publication. In crisis simulations, the self-healing mechanism more than doubled average Provenance Fidelity in difficult scenarios, and over 75% of human study participants preferred a report that explicitly stated failure over a fluent but fabricated consensus. Multi-agent debate (MAD) systems are increasingly deployed as decision pipelines, yet their final synthesis step can silently invent agreement that never occurred, creating a safety and trust gap. APG shifts provenance from passive logging to active conditional blocking, offering a concrete mitigation that could affect how LLM-based decision systems are audited and published in high-stakes domains. The APG is implemented as a deterministic state machine inserted as Phase 4 between the multi-agent debate and publication, treating the debate log as a hard constraint rather than a soft reference. When no reliable compromise exists, the gate blocks unsupported claims and generates divergence reports instead of forcing a summary, and the paper notes that users found the fabricated baseline consensus more fluent even while preferring the honest failure report.

**Background**: Multi-agent debate is a technique in which several large language model instances argue different positions and a summarizer condenses their exchange into a final answer, often improving reasoning and accuracy. Fabricated consensus occurs when that summarizer produces a confident, fluent conclusion that was never actually supported by the debate history, a failure mode distinct from simple factual hallucination. Provenance fidelity refers to how faithfully a summary's claims can be traced back to the underlying debate logs.

## [Uncertainty vs. Loss Signals Diverge Across Corruption Types in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31454) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper compares input-conditional uncertainty and prediction-label loss as corruption-detection signals in federated learning, testing them against additive image noise and persistent random label flips on ResNet-20 with CIFAR-10 and SVHN under non-IID Dirichlet partitions. It finds that prediction-label loss detects label flips with within-client per-sample AUC of 0.85 (CIFAR-10) and 0.95 (SVHN) while all uncertainty estimators stay at chance (0.49–0.50), whereas for image noise expected-entropy uncertainty rises above chance (0.67 and 0.66) with loss performing comparably (0.64 on both). The results suggest that robust federated learning data-quality assessment should match the detection signal to the specific corruption mode rather than relying on uncertainty alone, which could change how practitioners design anomaly detection and client-side data cleaning pipelines. This matters for anyone deploying FL in heterogeneous, non-IID environments where both noisy inputs and mislabeled data are common. The uncertainty signal is characterized using a learned aleatoric variance estimate together with Monte Carlo (MC) dropout variance and entropy measures, while the loss is computed against the supplied label. The authors note that the uncertainty advantage for image noise becomes more apparent as federation-wide corruption prevalence increases, and the pattern for label flips is consistent with the model remaining confident in the underlying image despite the supplied label being wrong.

## [PBOS: Preference-based opponent shaping for differentiable games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.03072) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes Preference-based Opponent Shaping (PBOS), a method that introduces a preference parameter into each agent's loss function so agents can directly consider opponents' losses when updating strategies. Preference parameters are updated concurrently with strategy learning, allowing adaptation to both cooperative and competitive differentiable games. Existing opponent modeling and shaping methods rely on simple predictions of opponent strategy changes and lack modeling of behavioral preferences like cooperation and competition, limiting them to predefined scenarios. PBOS addresses this generalization gap, which could benefit the multi-agent reinforcement learning community working on mixed cooperative-competitive settings. The preference parameter is embedded in the agent's loss function and updated jointly with strategy learning, and experiments across a variety of differentiable games show PBOS guides agents to learn appropriate preference parameters and achieve better reward distributions. The work is a technical contribution to MARL rather than a fundamental breakthrough, and its evaluation is limited to differentiable game settings.

Differentiable games are a formulation where agents' losses are differentiable with respect to their parameters, enabling gradient-based learning and analysis. Opponent shaping methods try to improve learning by accounting for how other agents update their strategies, but prior approaches model only simple strategy predictions rather than behavioral preferences.

A new arXiv paper evaluates a data-driven sparse sensing (DSS) workflow for optimal sensor placement and flow reconstruction in a 77-node urban drainage network, showing that monitoring just 3 nodes (4% of the network) achieves a median system-level Nash-Sutcliffe efficiency (NSE) of 0.791 across 225 held-out simulations. The pivoted QR-selected sensor sets matched the performance of Greedy D-optimal and genetic algorithm benchmarks without requiring iterative layout searches. The framework used a validated SWMM parameterization to build a spatial basis via singular value decomposition (SVD), selected rank-specific layouts using pivoted QR, and defined the reconstruction decoder. Performance was insensitive to multiplicative Gaussian noise but varied with the location of lost sensors, with sensitivity linked to energy-weighted modal exposure (Spearman correlation 0.781) and correlated with upstream drainage area, mean adjacent circular-conduit diameter, and nodal active flow fraction.

A new arXiv preprint (2603.05069v3) presents Jagarin, a three-layer architecture combining DAWN (Duty-Aware Wake Network), ARIA (Agent Relay Identity Architecture), and ACE (Agent-Centric Exchange) to let personal AI agents hibernate on mobile and wake only when needed. The paper describes a working Flutter prototype on Android that pairs these layers with an ephemeral cloud agent invoked only on user request. Mobile AI agents face a deployment paradox: always-on background execution drains batteries and violates platform background limits, while purely reactive agents miss time-sensitive obligations. Jagarin's structured hibernation and demand-driven wake could enable practical, privacy-preserving personal assistants that notify users at the right moment without constant resource consumption. DAWN is an on-device scoring engine that runs during the platform's periodic wake cycles and combines four signals—duty-typed optimal action windows, predicted user engagement, cost of delay, and cross-duty batching—with per-duty adaptive thresholds to decide whether to stay silent, nudge, or escalate. Behavioural signals, thresholds, and scoring never leave the device, and every record ARIA extracts is sealed to the device's public key so the relay holds only unreadable ciphertext; cloud model exposure is limited to parsing commercial email and user-initiated escalation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Personal AI agents on mobile must balance two conflicting constraints: persistent background execution drains battery and conflicts with platform background limits, while purely reactive agents miss time-sensitive obligations until the user remembers to ask. Jagarin addresses this by structuring agent hibernation and waking agents only when duty-aware scoring indicates action is warranted. The architecture separates concerns across three layers: on-device wake scoring (DAWN), email identity proxying (ARIA), and a machine-readable institution-to-agent protocol (ACE).

## [Statistical Priors Decouple Skill Selection as a Local Harness in Personal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05828) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.05828v2) proposes a lightweight local preference harness that strictly decouples statistical preference learning from semantic intent parsing in locally deployed personal agents. The approach uses localized statistical results to modulate the skill-selection decisions of a remote LLM, and evaluations show it achieves the lowest cumulative regret and highest test accuracy, significantly outperforming traditional memory-augmented agents. As LLM-based personal agents accumulate large skill libraries, learning implicit user preferences locally without heavy centralized computation is a critical bottleneck. This decoupled design could enable privacy-preserving, resource-constrained personal agents to personalize skill selection more effectively, an increasingly relevant direction as local-first AI assistants proliferate. The architecture separates statistical preference learning from semantic intent parsing: a local statistical component (bandit-style) chooses the preferred skill by default, while the remote LLM acts as an override probe when the user explicitly requests a specific skill. The paper reports lowest cumulative regret and highest test accuracy against memory-augmented baselines, though it remains a specialized preprint without community validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Personal agents built on large language models often rely on API-based remote models plus external skills, but local deployment constraints rule out complex centralized selection algorithms. Cumulative regret is a standard metric from online learning and recommendation systems that measures the accumulated loss from not always picking the optimal option. This paper applies that framing to skill selection, treating preference learning as a local statistical problem decoupled from the LLM's semantic understanding.

## [GPU Power Benchmark of 18 Local LLMs on Consumer Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00008) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.00008v2) presents a reproducible, hardware-level energy benchmark of 18 open-source LLMs ranging from 0.5B to 7B parameters, all run on a single consumer GPU (RTX 4060 Ti 16GB) via the Ollama inference engine. GPU power draw was sampled at 2 Hz using nvidia-smi across a fixed prompt set, measuring mean/peak power, energy per prompt (J/prompt), energy per output token (J/tok), and throughput (tok/s). Most LLM benchmarks focus only on accuracy, leaving the energy cost of local, on-premise inference poorly characterized; this work gives practitioners concrete data for choosing models when power and efficiency matter. It shows that architecture and quantization strategy, not parameter count alone, drive energy efficiency, which could shift how developers select models for consumer-grade local deployment. The most efficient models were qwen2.5:0.5b (0.2747 J/tok) and tinyllama:1.1b (0.3234 J/tok), both exceeding 325 tok/s, while the 7B-Mistral model consumed up to 8.6x more energy per token than the most efficient model. Notably, qwen3.5:0.8b(on) showed anomalously high per-prompt energy due to extended internal reasoning, highlighting the need to distinguish token generation modes in efficiency metrics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Locally deploying LLMs is increasingly popular for privacy and on-premise inference, but running them on consumer GPUs has real electricity costs that are rarely measured. Quantization is a model compression technique that converts weights and activations from high-precision to lower-precision representations, reducing memory use and speeding up inference. Ollama is a single-binary local LLM runner with a built-in model registry and automatic GPU detection, and nvidia-smi is NVIDIA's standard tool for monitoring GPU utilization, memory, temperature, and power draw.

## [Complex State Propagator Achieves Zero-Attenuation Deterministic State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03425) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.03425v3) introduces the Complex State Propagator (CSP), a minimalist recurrent model that operates purely on the complex phase manifold without intermediate output projections, amplitude modulations, or per-step non-linearities. Using an exact four-quadrant Coordinate-to-Phase (C-to-2) transformation based on the atan2(y, x) activation, a 3-layer CSP trained on length-16 inputs generalizes to a 4x longer out-of-distribution length of 64 on the Mod-3 tracking task, achieving nearly 100% validation accuracy and F1-score. The work claims that leading paradigms such as Transformers and Mamba suffer catastrophic out-of-distribution collapse when generalizing continuous deterministic state tracking to extended sequences, and that pure-phase complex propagation can beat much larger networks at a fraction of their size. If validated, this could offer a compact architectural alternative for tasks requiring long-horizon state tracking, though the result is currently limited to a single canonical synthetic task. The paper reports that the phase signal survives extreme depth with absolute zero informational attenuation, and that the C-to-2 transformation aligns the continuous optimization landscape with discrete cyclic groups. The headline result uses only a 3-layer hidden topology trained on short length-16 inputs and evaluated on the Mod-3 tracking task, so broader validation across real-world or more diverse benchmarks is not yet demonstrated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: State-space models and recurrent neural networks are designed to maintain an internal state over time, but they often degrade when asked to track deterministic patterns far beyond the sequence lengths seen during training, a failure mode known as out-of-distribution collapse. Complex-valued networks represent activations as complex numbers with magnitude and phase, which can encode cyclic or rotational structure more naturally than real-valued networks. The Mod-3 task is a standard synthetic benchmark in which a model must track a counter modulo 3, testing whether it can learn exact discrete state transitions rather than approximate them.

## [LapDDPM: Spectral Perturbation Diffusion for Robust Single-Cell Manifold Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13344) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers Lorenzo Bini and Stephane Marchand-Maillet introduced LapDDPM, a conditional graph diffusion probabilistic model that generates high-fidelity synthetic single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) data. The method adds a novel spectral adversarial perturbation mechanism that perturbs graph edge weights along principal spectral modes during training, and it was accepted at the 7th Conference on Health, Inference, and Learning (CHIL). High-quality synthetic scRNA-seq data is scarce and expensive to produce, so a model that reliably captures cellular differentiation topology could improve data augmentation, rare cell-type analysis, and downstream biological discovery. By framing generation as a robust inverse problem, LapDDPM also extends naturally to spatial transcriptomics and multi-modal data, broadening its potential impact in computational biology. LapDDPM combines graph-based inductive biases with score-based generative modeling and treats spectral perturbation as a Distributionally Robust Optimization (DRO) framework that enforces invariance to structural noise. Experiments on PBMC3K, Dentate Gyrus, HLCA, Visium, and 10x Multiome datasets show it outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in distribution matching, manifold preservation, and downstream utility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Single-cell RNA sequencing measures gene expression in individual cells, producing high-dimensional, sparse, and non-linear data that traces cellular differentiation manifolds. Diffusion probabilistic models generate data by gradually denoising random noise, while graph neural networks exploit relational structure between data points; LapDDPM merges both ideas for cellular graphs. Distributionally Robust Optimization (DRO) is a training paradigm that optimizes worst-case performance over a family of perturbed data distributions, which here provides robustness against technical noise.

## [Consist-Retinex Enables One-Step Low-Light Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08982) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers propose Consist-Retinex, a method that combines a Retinex Transformer Decomposition Network with two conditional consistency models trained via a Retinex-aware dual objective and adaptive noise-emphasized fixed-point sampling. It achieves the best VE-LOL-L scores among compared methods under one-step inference while substantially reducing sampling and consistency-stage training cost. Generative low-light enhancement methods typically rely on iterative sampling, which is impractical under strict latency budgets such as mobile or embedded deployment. By enabling high-quality one-step inference, this work could make Retinex-based enhancement viable for real-time applications. The paper provides an endpoint error bound, an anchoring-propagation result, and a high-noise sample-allocation analysis to explain why endpoint supervision and temporal consistency are complementary. The dual objective combines trajectory consistency with paired ground-truth component alignment, and the sampling rule concentrates supervision near the inference endpoint without discarding full-range noise coverage.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Retinex theory, inspired by human color vision, separates an image into reflectance and illumination components to enhance low-light scenes. Consistency models are a class of generative models that can map a noisy state directly to data in a single network evaluation, bypassing the iterative denoising of diffusion models. Directly adapting consistency models to Retinex-factorized enhancement is unstable because one-step inference is evaluated at the high-noise endpoint, where standard training schedules provide little supervision.

## [PromptCCZSL: Prompt-Based Continual Compositional Zero-Shot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09172) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces PromptCCZSL, the first prompt-based continual compositional zero-shot learning (CCZSL) framework, which adapts vision-language models to new attributes, objects, and their compositions without forgetting prior knowledge. It combines recency-weighted multi-teacher distillation, session-aware compositional prompts, session-agnostic attribute/object prompts, Cosine Anchor Loss, Orthogonal Projection Loss, and Intra-Session Diversity Loss, achieving substantial improvements on UT-Zappos and C-GQA benchmarks. This work addresses a key challenge in deploying vision-language models in dynamic real-world environments where new concepts continually emerge, enabling models to learn new attribute-object compositions without catastrophic forgetting. It sets a new benchmark for CCZSL in closed-world settings and could influence future research on continual learning and compositional generalization. The framework builds on a frozen VLM backbone and introduces a comprehensive protocol that jointly measures catastrophic forgetting and compositional generalization. It uses session-aware compositional prompts for new compositions and session-agnostic fusion for attribute and object prompts to maintain global semantic consistency, with CAL, OPL, and IDL stabilizing and enhancing representations.

**Background**: Compositional Zero-Shot Learning (CZSL) aims to recognize novel attribute-object compositions that never appeared together during training, by leveraging learned representations of seen compositions. Continual learning typically assumes disjoint classes across sessions, but in CCZSL, attributes and objects may reoccur while compositions remain unique, making it more complex. Vision-language models like CLIP provide strong multimodal representations, and prompt learning adapts them efficiently without full fine-tuning.

## [RAPTOR: Ridge-Adaptive Logistic Probes for LLM Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00158) ⭐️ 6.0/10

RAPTOR introduces a simple L2-regularized logistic probe whose validation-tuned ridge strength produces concept vectors from normalized weights, matching or exceeding strong baselines in accuracy while achieving competitive directional stability and substantially lower training cost. The paper also provides a mechanistic characterization of ridge logistic regression via the Convex Gaussian Min-max Theorem (CGMT) in an idealized Gaussian teacher-student model in the high-dimensional few-shot regime. This work matters because probe-then-steer pipelines are increasingly used to control LLM behavior, and RAPTOR offers a cheaper, more stable way to extract concept vectors, potentially improving interpretability and steering reliability for researchers and practitioners. It bridges empirical probing with theoretical understanding, which could guide better design of activation steering methods. RAPTOR uses an L2-regularized logistic probe with a validation-tuned ridge strength, and its concept vectors are derived from normalized weights. The theoretical analysis via CGMT explains how penalty strength mediates probe accuracy and concept-vector stability, with structural predictions qualitatively aligning with trends on real LLM embeddings.

**Background**: Probing in LLM interpretability involves training lightweight predictors on frozen layer representations to study what information is encoded. Probe-then-steer pipelines extract a concept vector from a probe and inject it via additive activation steering during the forward pass to control model behavior. Activation steering modifies internal activations to reliably alter outputs without retraining, using steering vectors to modulate properties like sentiment or truthfulness.

## [AFlow: Affective Flow Model for Emotional Support Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08826) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces the Affective Flow Language Model (AFlow), which models multi-turn emotional support conversation as an affective utility flow along dialogue trajectories, and proposes Affective Flow Preference Optimization (AFPO), a subpath-level flow-balance objective that propagates downstream preference signals to intermediate dialogue states. Experiments on the ExTES and ESConv benchmarks show consistent gains in strategy alignment, response diversity, and generation quality, with code released at github.com/chz2025/AffectiveFlow. Existing alignment methods for emotional support rely on sparse response-level preferences or dialogue-level outcomes, which give weak supervision for the sequential strategy decisions that matter most in multi-turn support. AFlow's process-based supervision could improve LLM-driven mental-health and empathetic dialogue systems, an area where poor strategy choices can harm users. AFlow searches diverse support trajectories and estimates the utility of intermediate dialogue states and candidate strategies, then uses a flow-balance objective defined over dialogue subpaths to learn strategy transitions consistent with full-dialogue outcomes. The approach is evaluated on ExTES and ESConv across different model environments and evaluation settings, though the abstract does not report specific quantitative numbers.

Large language models are increasingly used for this task, but aligning them typically relies on preference optimization methods like DPO that only compare whole responses, leaving intermediate strategy choices unsupervised. AFlow instead borrows the idea of flow-based modeling to assign credit to intermediate dialogue states along a trajectory.

## [Routing Games with Memory: Forgetful Equilibria and Recall Braess Paradox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11733) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces a design theory for routing games where travelers optimize over remembered or surfaced routes rather than a fixed action set, defining a stationary Forgetful Wardrop Equilibrium (FWE) and a salience-weighted stochastic user equilibrium. It proves existence and uniqueness of FWE under a contraction regime, shows exact equivalence between the micro and salience layers for last-choice memory (B=1), and defines a Recall Braess Paradox where improving recall increases equilibrium delay without changing physical capacity. This work bridges algorithmic game theory, transportation research, and human memory constraints, offering a tractable framework for designing information surfacing policies that can improve or worsen network performance. It matters for traffic management, navigation app design, and any system where users choose among a limited set of remembered options. The salience-weighted stochastic user equilibrium is the unique minimizer of a strictly convex potential, enabling clean optimization and implementability theory; the paper characterizes governed implementability under ratio budgets and affine tying constraints, and develops constructive algorithms for parallel and series-parallel networks. For larger memories, an LRU-to-TTL-to-salience approximation pipeline with contraction-based error bounds is provided, and the Recall Braess Paradox is shown to arise on every two-terminal network with at least two distinct s-t paths.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Routing games model how self-interested travelers choose paths in a congested network, with Wardrop equilibrium describing a state where no traveler can unilaterally improve their travel time. Classical models assume travelers know all routes, but in reality people rely on memory and limited information. The logit route choice model, based on discrete choice theory, captures probabilistic choice among alternatives, and Braess's paradox shows that adding capacity can sometimes increase congestion.

A new arXiv paper (2605.05236v2) proposes a topology-driven Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (TD-MARL) framework that coordinates multiple soft robots to avoid entanglement in highly constrained environments. Entanglement is a persistent failure mode for compliant robots operating in cluttered spaces, so a learning-based control scheme that explicitly mitigates it could improve reliability in precision manufacturing and other constrained industrial settings. The work also shows how topological invariants can be integrated into multi-agent reinforcement learning to stabilize training, an idea potentially transferable to other coordination problems. The critical network uses centralized learning so each agent can perceive other agents' policies through a shared topological state, while distributed execution removes the need for inter-robot communication and improves system reliability. An integrated topological security layer uses topological invariants to assess and mitigate entanglement risk, preventing policies from falling into local optima; validation is currently limited to simulation.

## [OASIS Stabilizes Attention-Residual Models Against Sinks and Outliers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17887) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces OASIS, an outlier- and sink-aware method that stabilizes dual-normalized attention-residual (AttnResidual) architectures using explicit Softmax1-based null routing at both token and depth levels. Across LLaMA-3.2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, and Phi-4, OASIS reduces maximum infinity norm by 81.75%, average kurtosis by 95.90%, lowers W8A8 perplexity by 82.00%, and improves W4A4 GSM8K Pass@1 by 42.11% on average. Attention sinks and activation outliers are major obstacles to low-bit quantization and stable inference in large language models, so a method that directly mitigates them could make aggressive quantization such as W4A4 more practical. The work also offers a theoretical characterization of sink-like attention concentration under dual normalization, which may guide future architecture design. OASIS builds on explicit Softmax1-based null routes at both token and depth levels, using token-level null evidence to downweight depth branches that exhibit stronger null behavior; the Softmax1 formulation is required for a non-zero null posterior. The method is validated against five baselines on three backbones and multiple language-modeling, reasoning, and long-context benchmarks, with code released at https://github.com/robinzixuan/OASIS.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: Transformer attention often develops "attention sinks," where a few tokens absorb a disproportionate share of attention mass, and "activation outliers," where a small number of activations have extreme magnitudes. These phenomena make low-bit quantization error-prone because a few large values dominate the dynamic range. AttnResidual adds a depth-wise normalization channel to improve inter-layer routing flexibility, but this can amplify sinks and outliers, motivating OASIS.

## [InMyStyle Uses LoRA on Small Qwen2.5 Models for Personal Style Rewriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29238) ⭐️ 6.0/10

InMyStyle is a privacy-first, single-user system that fine-tunes LoRA adapters on Qwen2.5 models ranging from 0.5B to 7B parameters to rewrite AI-edited text into an individual user's writing style without an instruction prompt at inference. In a case study with 219 evaluation pairs from one author's scientific writing, the automatic composite score plateaued across model sizes (Q=0.689–0.695), showing that small models are sufficient for the measured rewriting task. This work suggests that personalized style rewriting can be done locally on small, efficient models, which lowers the cost and privacy barriers for individual users who want AI-assisted writing that still sounds like them. It also challenges the assumption that larger models are always better for personalization tasks, pointing to efficiency trade-offs rather than quality gains as model size increases. All adapters were trained with the same rank-8, three-epoch recipe, and the gains favored content-preserving naturalization more than recovery of personal style, with authorship probabilities staying near the classifier's decision boundary (0.51–0.53) and stylometric improvement near zero. As a secondary evaluation, 400 ratings from five LLM judges gave InMyStyle outputs a mean perceived AI-ness score over 20% lower than their helper-generated inputs, but the study does not establish generalization across users.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes a pre-trained model's weights and injects small trainable rank decomposition matrices into each Transformer layer, greatly reducing the number of trainable parameters. Qwen2.5 is a family of open foundation models from Alibaba, available in dense and mixture-of-experts variants from 0.6B to 235B parameters. InMyStyle combines these by training per-user LoRA adapters on small Qwen2.5 models, using multiple local helper LLMs to construct paired training examples from a user's documents.

## [PILOT Framework Decouples Intention from Trajectory in World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06994) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces PILOT (Physical Inference for Latent Optimized Trajectories), a Representational Deduction (RD) framework that integrates motion chain-of-thought (CoT) guidance as a native capability of World Action Models. RD encourages the action branch to explicitly model potential state transition tokens, which are retained as CoT in the reasoning space to guide fine-grained motion trajectory generation. This work addresses a structural bottleneck in World Action Models where high-level physical condition evolution and low-level action trajectory generation become representationally entangled, weakening world evolution modeling for action generation. By decoupling these levels, PILOT reportedly improves success rate and generalization in complex robotic manipulation tasks, and offers an efficient few-shot real-robot fine-tuning strategy that could scale to mainstream WAM architectures. The abundant state transition supervision signals introduced by RD are said to alleviate sparse supervision in action generation, and the method is presented as a few-shot real-robot fine-tuning strategy with superior scalability for migration to mainstream WAM architectures. However, the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review, and the abstract is truncated, which limits clarity about experimental scope and baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 29, 04:00

**Background**: World Action Models (WAMs) aim to build a unified architecture that understands how the world state evolves and uses that understanding to guide generative motion planning. Existing visual branches in such models tend to predict static visual observations rather than the potential transition information that captures how world states change under motion interactions. Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting is a technique that encourages models to produce intermediate reasoning steps, and motion CoT extends this idea to trajectories by summarizing per-object direction, speed, and scale changes.
